<span class="atom-split" data-atom="as_you_like_it" data-title="AS YOU LIKE IT" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


AS YOU LIKE IT
As You Like It is first heard of in the Stationers’ Register on 4 August 1600, and was probably written not long before. In spite of its early entry for publication, it was not printed until 1623. This play, with its contrasts between [[settings/court|court]] and country, its bucolic as [[motifs/well|well]] as its aristocratic characters, its inset songs and poems, its predominantly woodland setting, its conscious artifice and its romantic ending, is the one in which Shakespeare makes most use of the conventions of pastoral literature, though he does not wholly endorse them.
The story of the [[motifs/love|love]] between a high-born maiden—[[characters/rosalind|Rosalind]]—oppressed by the uncle—[[characters/duke-frederick|Duke Frederick]]—who has usurped his elder brother’s dukedom, and [[characters/orlando|Orlando]], the third and youngest son of [[characters/duke-frederick|Duke Frederick]]’s old enemy Sir Rowland de Bois, himself oppressed by his tyrannical eldest brother [[characters/oliver|Oliver]], derives from Thomas Lodge’s Rosalynde, a prose romance interspersed with verses, which first appeared in 1590 and was several times reprinted. There are many indications that Shakespeare thought of the action as taking place in the Ardenne area of France, as in Rosalynde, even though there was also a Forest of Arden in Warwickshire. Like Lodge, Shakespeare counterpoints the developing [[motifs/love|love]] between [[characters/rosalind|Rosalind]]—who for much of the action is disguised as a boy, Ganymede—with the idealized pastoral romance of [[characters/silvius|Silvius]] and [[characters/phoebe|Phoebe]]; he adds the down-to-earth, unromantic affair between the jester [[characters/touchstone|Touchstone]] and Audrey. Once [[characters/rosalind|Rosalind]] and her cousin [[characters/celia|Celia]] (also disguised) reach the forest, plot is virtually suspended in favour of a series of scintillating conversations making much use of prose. The sudden flowering of [[motifs/love|love]] between [[characters/celia|Celia]] and [[characters/orlando|Orlando]]’s brother [[characters/oliver|Oliver]], newly converted to virtue, is based on Rosalynde, but Shakespeare alters the climax of the story, bringing Hymen, the god of [[motifs/marriage|marriage]], on stage to resolve all complications. As [[motifs/well|well]] as [[characters/touchstone|Touchstone]], Shakespeare added the [[concepts/melancholy|melancholy]] courtier [[characters/jaques|Jaques]], both of whom act as commentators, though from very different standpoints.
The first performances of Shakespeare’s text after his own [[motifs/time|time]] were given in 1740. It rapidly established itself in the theatrical repertoire, and has also been appreciated for its literary qualities. It has usually been played in picturesque settings, often since the late nineteenth century in the open air. [[characters/rosalind|Rosalind]] (written originally, of course, for a boy actor) is the dominant character, but other roles, especially [[characters/jaques|Jaques]], [[characters/touchstone|Touchstone]], Audrey, Corin, and—in his single scene—William, have proved particularly effective when played by performers with a strong sense of their latent individuality.
THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY
[[characters/duke-senior|DUKE SENIOR]], living in banishment
[[characters/rosalind|ROSALIND]], his daughter, later disguised as Ganymede
TWO PAGES

[[characters/duke-frederick|DUKE FREDERICK]]
[[characters/celia|CELIA]], his daughter, later disguised as Aliena
LE BEAU, a courtier attending on him
CHARLES, [[characters/duke-frederick|Duke Frederick]]’s wrestler
[[characters/touchstone|TOUCHSTONE]], a jester

[[characters/oliver|OLIVER]], eldest son of Sir Rowland de Bois
ADAM, a former servant of Sir Rowland

DENIS, [[characters/oliver|Oliver]]’s servant

SIR [[characters/oliver|OLIVER]] MARTEXT, a country clergyman
CORIN, an old shepherd
[[characters/silvius|SILVIUS]], a young shepherd, in [[motifs/love|love]] with [[characters/phoebe|Phoebe]]
[[characters/phoebe|PHOEBE]], a shepherdess
WILLIAM, a countryman, in [[motifs/love|love]] with Audrey
AUDREY, a goatherd, betrothed to [[characters/touchstone|Touchstone]]

HYMEN, god of [[motifs/marriage|marriage]]

Lords, pages, and other attendants

As You Like It

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and Adam |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | As I remember, Adam, it was upon this fashion bequeathed me by will but poor a thousand crowns, and, as thou sayst, charged my brother on his blessing to breed me [[motifs/well\|well]]—and there begins my sadness. My brother [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] he keeps at school, and report speaks goldenly of his profit. For my part, he keeps me rustically at [[motifs/home\|home]]—or, to speak more properly, stays me here at [[motifs/home\|home]] unkept; for call you that keeping for a gentleman of my birth, that differs not from the stalling of an ox? His horses are bred better, for besides that they are fair with their feeding, they are taught their manège, and to that end riders dearly hired. But I, his brother, gain nothing under him but growth, for the which his [[motifs/animals\|animals]] on his dunghills are as much bound to him as I. Besides this nothing that he so plentifully gives me, the something that [[concepts/nature\|nature]] gave me his countenance seems to take from me. He lets me feed with his hinds, bars me the place of a brother, and as much as in him lies, mines my gentility with my [[concepts/education\|education]]. This is it, Adam, that grieves me; and the spirit of my father, which I think is within me, begins to mutiny against this servitude. I will no longer endure it, though yet I know no wise remedy how to avoid it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] |
| ADAM | Yonder comes my master, your brother. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Go apart, Adam, and thou shalt hear how he will shake me up.<br>Adam stands aside |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Now, sir, what make you here? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Nothing. I am not taught to make anything. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | What mar you then, sir? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Marry, sir, I am helping you to mar that which God made, a poor unworthy brother of yours, with idleness. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Marry, sir, be better employed, and be nought awhile. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Shall I keep your hogs, and eat husks with them? What prodigal portion have I spent, that I should come to such penury? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Know you where you are, sir? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | O sir, very [[motifs/well\|well]]; here in your orchard. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Know you before whom, sir? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Ay, better than him I am before knows me. I know you are my eldest brother, and in the gentle condition of [[motifs/blood\|blood]] you should so know me. The courtesy of nations allows you my better, in that you are the first-born; but the same tradition takes not away my [[motifs/blood\|blood]], were there twenty brothers betwixt us. I have as much of my father in me as you, albeit I confess your coming before me is nearer to his reverence. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | (assailing him) What, boy! |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | (seizing him by the throat) Come, come, elder brother, you are too young in this. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Wilt thou lay hands on me, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I am no villein. I am the youngest son of Sir Rowland de Bois. He was my father, and he is thrice a [[archetypes/villain\|villain]] that says such a father begot villeins. Wert thou not my brother, I would not take this hand from thy throat till this other had pulled out thy tongue for saying so. Thou hast railed on thyself. |
| ADAM | (coming forward) Sweet masters, be patient. For your father’s remembrance, be at accord. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Let me go, I say. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I will not till I please. You shall hear me. My father charged you in his will to give me good [[concepts/education\|education]]. You have trained me like a peasant, obscuring and hiding from me all gentleman-like qualities. The spirit of my father grows strong in me, and I will no longer endure it. Therefore allow me such exercises as may become a gentleman, or give me the poor allottery my father left me by testament. With that I will go buy my fortunes. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | And what wilt thou do—beg when that is spent? [[motifs/well\|Well]], sir, get you in. I will not long be troubled with you. You shall have some part of your will. I pray you, leave me. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I will no further offend you than becomes me for my good. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | (to Adam) Get you with him, you old dog. |
| ADAM | Is ‘old dog’ my reward? Most true, I have lost my teeth in your service. God be with my old master, he would not have spoke such a word. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and Adam |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Is it even so? Begin you to grow upon me? I will physic your rankness, and yet give no thousand crowns neither. Holla, Denis! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Denis |
| DENIS | Calls your worship? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Was not Charles, the Duke’s wrestler, here to speak with me? |
| DENIS | So please you, he is here at the door, and importunes access to you. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Call him in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Denis |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | ’Twill be a good way. And tomorrow the wrestling is. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Charles |
| CHARLES | Good morrow to your worship. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Good Monsieur Charles—what’s the new news at the new [[settings/court\|court]]? |
| CHARLES | There’s no news at the [[settings/court\|court]], sir, but the old news: that is, the old Duke is banished by his younger brother, the new Duke, and three or four loving lords have put themselves into voluntary [[concepts/exile\|exile]] with him, whose lands and revenues enrich the new Duke; therefore he gives them good leave to wander. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Can you tell if [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], the Duke’s daughter, be banished with her father? |
| CHARLES | O no; for the Duke’s daughter her cousin so loves her, being ever from their cradles bred together, that she would have followed her [[concepts/exile\|exile]], or have died to stay behind her. She is at the [[settings/court\|court]], and no less beloved of her uncle than his own daughter; and never two ladies loved as they do. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Where will the old Duke live? |
| CHARLES | They say he is already in the forest of Ardenne, and a many merry men with him; and there they live like the old [[characters/robin-hood\|Robin Hood]] of England. They say many young gentlemen flock to him every day, and fleet the [[motifs/time\|time]] carelessly, as they did in the golden world. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | What, you wrestle tomorrow before the new Duke? |
| CHARLES | Marry do I, sir, and I came to acquaint you with a matter. I am given, sir, secretly to understand that your younger brother, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], hath a disposition to come in disguised against me to try a fall. Tomorrow, sir, I wrestle for my credit, and he that escapes me without some broken limb, shall acquit him [[motifs/well\|well]]. Your brother is but young and tender, and for your [[motifs/love\|love]] I would be loath to foil him, as I must for my own [[concepts/honour\|honour]] if he come in. Therefore out of my [[motifs/love\|love]] to you I came hither to acquaint you withal, that either you might stay him from his intendment, or brook such disgrace [[motifs/well\|well]] as he shall run into, in that it is a thing of his own search, and altogether against my will. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Charles, I thank thee for thy [[motifs/love\|love]] to me, which thou shalt find I will most kindly requite. I had myself notice of my brother’s purpose herein, and have by underhand means laboured to dissuade him from it; but he is resolute. I’ll tell thee, Charles, it is the stubbornest young fellow of France, full of [[concepts/ambition\|ambition]], an envious emulator of every man’s good parts, a secret and villainous contriver against me his natural brother. Therefore use thy discretion. I had as lief thou didst break his neck as his finger. And thou wert best look to’t; for if thou dost him any slight disgrace, or if he do not mightily grace himself on thee, he will practise against thee by [[motifs/poison\|poison]], entrap thee by some treacherous device, and never leave thee till he hath ta’en thy life by some indirect means or other. For I assure thee—and almost with [[motifs/tears\|tears]] I speak it—there is not one so young and so villainous this day living. I speak but brotherly of him, but should I anatomize him to thee as he is, I must blush and weep, and thou must look pale and [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]]. |
| CHARLES | I am heartily glad I came hither to you. If he come tomorrow I’ll give him his payment. If ever he go alone again, I’ll never wrestle for prize more. And so God keep your worship. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Farewell, good Charles. Exit Charles Now will I stir this gamester. I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] I shall see an end of him, for my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]—yet I know not why—hates nothing more than he. Yet he’s gentle; never schooled, and yet learned; full of noble device; of all sorts enchantingly beloved; and, indeed, so much in the heart of the world, and especially of my own people, who best know him, that I am altogether misprized. But it shall not be so long. This wrestler shall clear all. Nothing remains but that I kindle the boy thither, which now I’ll go about. Exit |

### Atto 1, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I pray thee [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], sweet my coz, be merry. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Dear [[characters/celia\|Celia]], I show more mirth than I am mistress of; and would you yet I were merrier? Unless you could teach me to forget a banished father you must not learn me how to remember any extraordinary pleasure. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Herein I see thou lovest me not with the full weight that I [[motifs/love\|love]] thee. If my uncle, thy banished father, had banished thy uncle, the Duke my father, so thou hadst been still with me I could have taught my [[motifs/love\|love]] to take thy father for mine. So wouldst thou, if the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] of thy [[motifs/love\|love]] to me were so righteously tempered as mine is to thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will forget the condition of my estate to rejoice in yours. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | You know my father hath no [[archetypes/child\|child]] but I, nor none is like to have. And truly, when he dies thou shalt be his heir; for what he hath taken away from thy father perforce, I will render thee again in affection. By mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] I will, and when I break that oath, let me turn [[archetypes/monster\|monster]]. Therefore, my sweet [[motifs/rose\|Rose]], my dear [[motifs/rose\|Rose]], be merry. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | From henceforth I will, coz, and devise sports. Let me see, what think you of falling in [[motifs/love\|love]]? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Marry, I prithee do, to make sport withal; but [[motifs/love\|love]] no man in good earnest, nor no further in sport neither than with safety of a pure blush thou mayst in [[concepts/honour\|honour]] come off again. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | What shall be our sport; then? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Let us sit and mock the good housewife Fortune from her [[motifs/wheel\|wheel]], that her gifts may henceforth be bestowed equally. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I would we could do so, for her benefits are mightily misplaced; and the bountiful blind woman doth most mistake in her gifts to women. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | ’Tis true; for those that she makes fair she scarce makes honest, and those that she makes honest she makes very ill-favouredly. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, now thou goest from Fortune’s office to [[concepts/nature\|Nature]]’s. Fortune reigns in gifts of the world, not in the lineaments of [[concepts/nature\|nature]]. Enter [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | No. When [[concepts/nature\|Nature]] hath made a fair creature, may she not by Fortune fall into the [[motifs/fire\|fire]]? Though [[concepts/nature\|Nature]] hath given us wit to flout at Fortune, hath not Fortune sent in this [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] to cut off the argument? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Indeed, there is Fortune too hard for [[concepts/nature\|Nature]], when Fortune makes [[concepts/nature\|Nature]]’s natural the cutter-off of [[concepts/nature\|Nature]]’s wit. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Peradventure this is not Fortune’s work, neither, but [[concepts/nature\|Nature]]’s, who perceiveth our natural wits too dull to reason of such goddesses, and hath sent this natural for our whetstone; for always the dullness of the [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] is the whetstone of the wits. How now, wit: whither wander you? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Mistress, you must come away to your father. [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] Were you made the messenger? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | No, by mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]], but I was bid to come for you. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Where learned you that oath, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Of a certain knight that swore ‘by his [[concepts/honour\|honour]]’ they were good pancakes, and swore ’by his [[concepts/honour\|honour]]’ the mustard was naught. Now I’ll stand to it the pancakes were naught and the mustard was good, and yet was not the knight forsworn. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | How prove you that in the great heap of your knowledge? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, marry, now unmuzzle your wisdom. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Stand you both forth now. Stroke your chins, and swear by your beards that I am a knave. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | By our beards—if we had them—thou art. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | By my knavery—if I had it—then I were; but if you swear by that that is not, you are not forsworn. No more was this knight, swearing by his [[concepts/honour\|honour]], for he never had any; or if he had, he had sworn it away before ever he saw those pancakes or that mustard. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Prithee, who is’t that thou meanest? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | One that old Frederick, your father, loves.<br>[[[characters/celia\|CELIA]]] My father’s [[motifs/love\|love]] is enough to [[concepts/honour\|honour]] him. Enough, speak no more of him; you’ll be whipped for taxation one of these days. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | The more pity that fools may not speak wisely what wise men do foolishly. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | By my troth, thou sayst true; for since the little wit that fools have was silenced, the little foolery that wise men have makes a great show. Here comes Monsieur Le Beau. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Le Beau |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With his mouth full of news. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Which he will put on us as pigeons feed their young. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Then shall we be news-crammed. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | All the better: we shall be the more marketable. Bonjour, Monsieur Le Beau, what’s the news? |
| LE BEAU | Fair princess, you have lost much good sport. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Sport? Of what colour? |
| LE BEAU | What colour, madam? How shall I answer you? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | As wit and fortune will. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Or as the destinies decrees. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]] said. That was laid on with a trowel. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Nay, if I keep not my rank— |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Thou losest thy old smell. |
| LE BEAU | You amaze me, ladies. I would have told you of good wrestling, which you have lost the sight of. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Yet tell us the manner of the wrestling. |
| LE BEAU | I will tell you the beginning, and if it please your ladyships you may see the end, for the best is yet to do, and here, where you are, they are coming to perform it. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], the beginning that is dead and buried. |
| LE BEAU | There comes an old man and his three sons— |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I could match this beginning with an old tale. |
| LE BEAU | Three proper young men, of excellent growth and presence. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With bills on their necks: ‘Be it known unto all men by these presents’— |
| LE BEAU | The eldest of the three wrestled with Charles, the Duke’s wrestler, which Charles in a moment threw him, and broke three of his ribs, that there is little [[concepts/hope\|hope]] of life in him. So he served the second, and so the third. Yonder they lie, the poor old man their father making such pitiful dole over them that all the beholders take his part with weeping. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Alas! |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | But what is the sport, monsieur, that the ladies have lost? |
| LE BEAU | Why, this that I speak of. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Thus men may grow wiser every day. It is the first [[motifs/time\|time]] that ever I heard breaking of ribs was sport for ladies. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Or I, I promise thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But is there any else longs to see this broken music in his sides? Is there yet another dotes upon rib-breaking? Shall we see this wrestling, cousin? |
| LE BEAU | You must if you stay here, for here is the place appointed for the wrestling, and they are ready to perform it. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Yonder sure they are coming. Let us now stay and see it. |
| *(didascalia)* | Flourish. Enter [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], Lords, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], Charles, and attendants |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Come on. Since the youth will not be entreated, his own peril on his forwardness. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Is yonder the man? |
| LE BEAU | Even he, madam. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Alas, he is too young. Yet he looks successfully. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | How now, daughter and cousin; are you crept hither to see the wrestling? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, my liege, so please you give us leave. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | You will take little delight in it, I can tell you, there is such odds in the man. In pity of the challenger’s youth I would fain dissuade him, but he will not be entreated. Speak to him, ladies; see if you can move him. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Call him hither, good Monsieur Le Beau. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Do SO. I’ll not be by.<br>He stands aside |
| LE BEAU | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Monsieur the challenger, the Princess calls for you. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I attend them with all respect and duty. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Young man, have you challenged Charles the wrestler? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | No, fair Princess. He is the general challenger; I come but in as others do, to try with him the strength of my youth. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Young gentleman, your spirits are too bold for your years. You have seen cruel proof of this man’s strength. If you saw yourself with your eyes, or knew yourself with your judgement, the [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of your adventure would counsel you to a more equal enterprise. We pray you for your own sake to embrace your own safety and give over this attempt. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Do, young sir. Your reputation shall not therefore be misprized. We will make it our suit to the Duke that the wrestling might not go forward. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I beseech you, punish me not with your hard thoughts, wherein I confess me much guilty to deny so fair and excellent ladies anything. But let your fair eyes and gentle wishes go with me to my trial, wherein if I be foiled, there is but one shamed that was never gracious, if killed, but one dead that is willing to be so. I shall do my friends no wrong, for I have none to lament me; the world no injury, for in it I have nothing. Only in the world I fill up a place which may be better supplied when I have made it empty. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | The little strength that I have, I would it were with you. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | And mine, to eke out hers. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. Pray heaven I be deceived in you. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Your heart’s desires be with you. |
| CHARLES | Come, where is this young gallant that is so desirous to lie with his mother earth? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Ready, sir; but his will hath in it a more modest working. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | You shall try but one fall. |
| CHARLES | No, I warrant your grace you shall not entreat him to a second that have so mightily persuaded him from a first. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | You mean to mock me after; you should not have mocked me before. But come your ways. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Now Hercules be thy [[characters/speed\|speed]], young man! |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I would I were invisible, to catch the strong fellow by the leg.<br>Charles and [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] wrestle |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O excellent young man! |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | If I had a thunderbolt in mine eye, I can tell who should down.<br>[[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] throws Charles. Shout |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | No more, no more. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Yes, I beseech your grace.<br>I am not yet [[motifs/well\|well]] breathed. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | How dost thou, Charles? |
| LE BEAU | He cannot speak, my lord. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Bear him away.<br>Attendants carry Charles off<br>What is thy name, young man? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], my liege, the youngest son of Sir Rowland de Bois. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | I would thou hadst been son to some man else.<br>The world esteemed thy father honourable,<br>But I did find him still mine enemy.<br>Thou shouldst have better pleased me with this deed<br>Hadst thou descended from another house.<br>But fare thee [[motifs/well\|well]], thou art a gallant youth.<br>I would thou hadst told me of another father. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], Le Beau, [[[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]],] Lords, and attendants |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | (to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]])<br>Were I my father, coz, would I do this? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I am more proud to be Sir Rowland’s son,<br>His youngest son, and would not change that calling<br>To be adopted heir to Frederick. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | My father loved Sir Rowland as his [[motifs/soul\|soul]],<br>And all the world was of my father’s mind.<br>Had I before known this young man his son<br>I should have given him [[motifs/tears\|tears]] unto entreaties<br>Ere he should thus have ventured. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Gentle cousin,<br>Let us go thank him, and encourage him.<br>My father’s rough and envious disposition<br>Sticks me at heart.—Sir, you have [[motifs/well\|well]] deserved.<br>If you do keep your promises in [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>But justly, as you have exceeded all promise,<br>Your mistress shall be happy. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (giving him a chain from her neck) Gentleman, Wear this for me—one out of suits with fortune, That could give more but that her hand lacks means. Shall we go, coz? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Ay. Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]], fair gentleman.<br>[[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] turn to go |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | (aside)<br>Can I not say ‘I thank you’? My better parts<br>Are all thrown down, and that which here stands up<br>Is but a quintain, a mere lifeless block. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/celia\|Celia]])<br>He calls us back. My [[concepts/pride\|pride]] fell with my fortunes,<br>I’ll ask him what he would.—Did you call, sir?<br>Sir, you have wrestled [[motifs/well\|well]], and overthrown<br>More than your enemies. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Will you go, coz? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Have with you. (To [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What passion hangs these weights upon my tongue?<br>I cannot speak to her, yet she urged conference. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Le Beau |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | O poor [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]! Thou art overthrown.<br>Or Charles or something weaker masters thee. |
| LE BEAU | Good sir, I do in [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]] counsel you<br>To leave this place. Albeit you have deserved<br>High commendation, true applause, and [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Yet such is now the Duke’s condition<br>That he misconsters all that you have done.<br>The Duke is humorous. What he is indeed<br>More suits you to conceive than I to speak of. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I thank you, sir. And pray you tell me this,<br>Which of the two was daughter of the Duke<br>That here was at the wrestling? |
| LE BEAU | Neither his daughter, if we judge by manners—<br>But yet indeed the shorter is his daughter.<br>The other is daughter to the banished Duke,<br>And here detained by her usurping uncle<br>To keep his daughter company, whose loves<br>Are dearer than the natural bond of sisters.<br>But I can tell you that of late this Duke<br>Hath ta‘en displeasure ’gainst his gentle niece,<br>Grounded upon no other argument<br>But that the people praise her for her virtues<br>And pity her for her good father’s sake.<br>And, on my life, his malice ’gainst the lady<br>Will suddenly break forth. Sir, fare you [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>Hereafter, in a better world than this,<br>I shall [[motifs/desire\|desire]] more [[motifs/love\|love]] and knowledge of you. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I rest much bounden to you. Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Le Beau |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Thus must I from the smoke into the smother,<br>From [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] Duke unto a [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] brother.—<br>But heavenly [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]! Exit<br><br>13. Enter [[characters/celia\|Celia]] and [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Why cousin, why [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]—Cupid have [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], not a word? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Not one to throw at a dog. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | No, thy words are too precious to be cast away upon curs. Throw some of them at me. Come, lame me with reasons. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Then there were two cousins laid up, when the one should be lamed with reasons and the other mad without any. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | But is all this for your father? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | No, some of it is for my [[archetypes/child\|child]]’s father. O how full of briers is this working-day world! |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | They are but burs, cousin, thrown upon thee in holiday foolery. If we walk not in the trodden paths our very petticoats will catch them. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I could shake them off my coat. These burs are in my heart. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Hem them away. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I would try, if I could cry ‘hem’ and have him. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Come, come, wrestle with thy affections. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O, they take the part of a better wrestler than myself. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O, a good wish upon you! You will try in [[motifs/time\|time]], in despite of a fall. But turning these jests out of service, let us talk in good earnest. Is it possible on such a sudden you should fall into so strong a liking with old Sir Rowland’s youngest son? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | The Duke my father loved his father dearly. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Doth it therefore ensue that you should [[motifs/love\|love]] his son dearly? By this kind of chase I should hate him, for my father hated his father dearly; yet I hate not [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | No, [[concepts/faith\|faith]], hate him not, for my sake. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Why should I not? Doth he not deserve [[motifs/well\|well]]? Enter [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], with Lords |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Let me [[motifs/love\|love]] him for that, and do you [[motifs/love\|love]] him because I do. Look, here comes the Duke. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | With his eyes full of anger. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | (to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]) Mistress, dispatch you with your safest haste, And get you from our [[settings/court\|court]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Me, uncle? |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | You, cousin. Within these ten days if that thou beest found So near our public [[settings/court\|court]] as twenty miles, Thou diest for it. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I do beseech your grace<br>Let me the knowledge of my fault bear with me.<br>If with myself I hold intelligence,<br>Or have acquaintance with mine own desires,<br>If that I do not dream, or be not frantic—<br>As I do trust I am not—then, dear uncle,<br>Never so much as in a thought unborn<br>Did I offend your highness. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Thus do all traitors.<br>If their purgation did consist in words<br>They are as [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]] as grace itself.<br>Let it suffice thee that I trust thee not. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Yet your mistrust cannot make me a traitor.<br>Tell me whereon the likelihood depends? |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Thou art thy father’s daughter—there’s enough. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | So was I when your highness took his dukedom;<br>So was I when your highness banished him.<br>Treason is not inherited, my lord,<br>Or if we did derive it from our friends,<br>What’s that to me? My father was no traitor.<br>Then, good my liege, mistake me not so much<br>To think my [[concepts/poverty\|poverty]] is treacherous. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Dear sovereign, hear me speak. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Ay, [[characters/celia\|Celia]], we stayed her for your sake,<br>Else had she with her father ranged along. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I did not then entreat to have her stay.<br>It was your pleasure, and your own remorse.<br>I was too young that [[motifs/time\|time]] to value her,<br>But now I know her. If she be a traitor,<br>Why, so am I. We still have slept together,<br>[[motifs/rose\|Rose]] at an instant, learned, played, eat together,<br>And wheresoe’er we went, like Juno’s swans<br>Still we went coupled and inseparable. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | She is too subtle for thee, and her smoothness,<br>Her very [[motifs/silence\|silence]], and her patience<br>Speak to the people, and they pity her.<br>Thou art a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. She robs thee of thy name,<br>And thou wilt show more bright and seem more<br>virtuous<br>When she is gone. Then open not thy lips.<br>Firm and irrevocable is my doom<br>Which I have passed upon her. She is banished. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Pronounce that sentence then on me, my liege.<br>I cannot live out of her company. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | You are a foot.—You, niece, provide yourself.<br>If you outstay the [[motifs/time\|time]], upon mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>And in the [[concepts/greatness\|greatness]] of my word, you die. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], with Lords |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O my poor [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], whither wilt thou go?<br>Wilt thou change fathers? I will give thee mine.<br>I charge thee, be not thou more grieved than I am. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I have more cause. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Thou hast not, cousin.<br>Prithee, be cheerful. Know’st thou not the Duke<br>Hath banished me, his daughter? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | That he hath not. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | No, hath not? [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], lack’st thou then the [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>Which teacheth thee that thou and I am one?<br>Shall we be sundered? Shall we part, sweet girl?<br>No. Let my father seek another heir.<br>Therefore devise with me how we may fly,<br>Whither to go, and what to bear with us,<br>And do not seek to take your change upon you,<br>To bear your griefs yourself, and leave me out.<br>For by this heaven, now at our sorrows pale,<br>Say what thou canst, I’ll go along with thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why, whither shall we go? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | To seek my uncle in the forest of Ardenne. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Alas, what danger will it be to us,<br>Maids as we are, to travel forth so far!<br>[[concepts/beauty\|Beauty]] provoketh thieves sooner than gold. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I’ll put myself in poor and mean attire,<br>And with a kind of umber smirch my face.<br>The like do you, so shall we pass along<br>And never stir assailants. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Were it not better,<br>Because that I am more than common tall,<br>That I did suit me all points like a man,<br>A gallant curtal-axe upon my thigh,<br>A boar-spear in my hand, and in my heart,<br>Lie there what hidden woman’s [[concepts/fear\|fear]] there will.<br>We’ll have a swashing and a martial outside,<br>As many other mannish cowards have,<br>That do outface it with their semblances. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | What shall I call thee when thou art a man? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I’ll have no worse a name than Jove’s own page,<br>And therefore look you call me Ganymede.<br>But what will you be called? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Something that hath a reference to my state.<br>No longer [[characters/celia\|Celia]], but Aliena. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But cousin, what if we essayed to steal<br>The clownish [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] out of your father’s [[settings/court\|court]].<br>Would he not be a comfort to our travel? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | He’ll go along o’er the wide world with me.<br>Leave me alone to woo him. Let’s away,<br>And get our jewels and our wealth together,<br>Devise the fittest [[motifs/time\|time]] and safest way<br>To hide us from pursuit that will be made<br>After my flight. Now go we in content,<br>To liberty, and not to banishment. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/duke-senior\|Duke Senior]], Amiens, and two or three Lords dressed as foresters |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Now, my co-mates and brothers in [[concepts/exile\|exile]],<br>Hath not old custom made this life more sweet<br>Than that of painted pomp? Are not these woods<br>More free from peril than the envious [[settings/court\|court]]?<br>Here feel we not the penalty of Adam,<br>The [[motifs/seasons\|seasons]]’ difference, as the icy fang<br>And churlish chiding of the [[motifs/winter\|winter]]’s [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>Which when it bites and blows upon my [[concepts/body\|body]]<br>Even till I shrink with cold, I smile, and say<br>‘This is no flattery. These are counsellors<br>That feelingly persuade me what I am.’<br>Sweet are the uses of adversity<br>Which, like the toad, ugly and venomous,<br>Wears yet a precious [[motifs/jewel\|jewel]] in his head;<br>And this our life, exempt from public haunt,<br>Finds tongues in trees, books in the running brooks,<br>Sermons in stones, and good in everything. |
| AMIENS | I would not change it. Happy is your grace<br>That can translate the stubbornness of fortune<br>Into so quiet and so sweet a style. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Come, shall we go and kill us venison?<br>And yet it irks me the poor dappled fools,<br>Being native burghers of this [[motifs/desert\|desert]] [[motifs/city\|city]],<br>Should in their own confines with forked heads<br>Have their round haunches gored. |
| FIRST LORD | Indeed, my lord,<br>The [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] grieves at that,<br>And in that kind swears you do more usurp<br>Than doth your brother that hath banished you.<br>Today my lord of Amiens and myself<br>Did steal behind him as he lay along<br>Under an oak, whose antic root peeps out<br>Upon the brook that brawls along this wood,<br>To the which place a poor sequestered stag<br>That from the hunter’s aim had ta‘en a hurt<br>Did come to languish. And indeed, my lord,<br>The wretched animal heaved forth such groans<br>That their discharge did stretch his leathern coat<br>Almost to bursting, and the big round [[motifs/tears\|tears]]<br>Coursed one another down his [[archetypes/innocent\|innocent]] nose<br>In piteous chase. And thus the hairy [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],<br>Much marked of the [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]],<br>Stood on th’extremest verge of the swift brook,<br>Augmenting it with [[motifs/tears\|tears]]. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | But what said [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]]?<br>Did he not moralize this spectacle? |
| FIRST LORD | O yes, into a thousand similes.<br>First, for his weeping into the needless stream;<br>‘Poor deer,’ quoth he, ‘thou mak’st a testament<br>As worldlings do, giving thy sum of more<br>To that which had too much.’ Then being there<br>alone,<br>Left and abandoned of his velvet friend,<br>“Tis right,’ quoth he, ‘thus misery doth part<br>The flux of company.’ Anon a careless herd<br>Full of the pasture jumps along by him<br>And never stays to greet him. ‘Ay,’ quoth [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]],<br>‘Sweep on, you fat and greasy citizens,<br>’Tis just the fashion. Wherefore should you look<br>Upon that poor and broken bankrupt there?’<br>Thus most invectively he pierceth through<br>The [[concepts/body\|body]] of the country, [[motifs/city\|city]], [[settings/court\|court]],<br>Yea, and of this our life, swearing that we<br>Are mere usurpers, tyrants, and what’s worse,<br>To fright the [[motifs/animals\|animals]] and to kill them up<br>In their assigned and native dwelling place. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | And did you leave him in this contemplation? |
| SECOND LORD | We did, my lord, weeping and commenting<br>Upon the sobbing deer. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Show me the place.<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] to cope him in these sullen fits,<br>For then he’s full of matter. |
| FIRST LORD | I’ll bring you to him straight. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], with Lords |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Can it be possible that no man saw them?<br>It cannot be. Some villains of my [[settings/court\|court]]<br>Are of consent and sufferance in this. |
| FIRST LORD | I cannot hear of any that did see her.<br>The ladies her attendants of her chamber<br>Saw her abed, and in the morning early<br>They found the bed untreasured of their mistress. |
| SECOND LORD | My lord, the roynish clown at whom so oft<br>Your grace was wont to laugh is also missing.<br>Hisperia, the Princess’ gentlewoman,<br>Confesses that she secretly o’erheard<br>Your daughter and her cousin much commend<br>The parts and graces of the wrestler<br>That did but lately foil the sinewy Charles,<br>And she believes wherever they are gone<br>That youth is surely in their company. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Send to his brother; fetch that gallant hither.<br>If he be absent, bring his brother to me,<br>I’ll make him find him. Do this suddenly,<br>And let not search and [[historical-references/inquisition\|inquisition]] quail<br>To bring again these foolish runaways. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and Adam, meeting |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Who’s there? |
| ADAM | What, my young master, O my gentle master,<br>O my sweet master, O you [[motifs/memory\|memory]]<br>Of old Sir Rowland, why, what make you here!<br>Why are you virtuous? Why do people [[motifs/love\|love]] you?<br>And wherefore are you gentle, strong, and valiant?<br>Why would you be so fond to overcome<br>The bonny prizer of the humorous Duke?<br>Your praise is come too swiftly [[motifs/home\|home]] before you.<br>Know you not, master, to some kind of men<br>Their graces serve them but as enemies?<br>No more do yours. Your virtues, gentle master,<br>Are sanctified and holy traitors to you.<br>O, what a world is this, when what is comely<br>Envenoms him that bears it! |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Why, what’s the matter? |
| ADAM | O, unhappy youth,<br>Come not within these doors. Within this roof<br>The enemy of all your graces lives,<br>Your brother—no, no brother—yet the son—<br>Yet not the son, I will not call him son—<br>Of him I was about to call his father,<br>Hath heard your praises, and this [[motifs/night\|night]] he means<br>To burn the lodging where you use to lie,<br>And you within it. If he fail of that,<br>He will have other means to cut you off.<br>I overheard him and his practices.<br>This is no place, this house is but a butchery.<br>Abhor it, [[concepts/fear\|fear]] it, do not enter it. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Why, whither, Adam, wouldst thou have me go? |
| ADAM | No matter whither, so you come not here. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What, wouldst thou have me go and beg my food,<br>Or with a base and boisterous sword enforce<br>A thievish living on the common [[motifs/road\|road]]?<br>This I must do, or know not what to do.<br>Yet this I will not do, do how I can.<br>I rather will subject me to the malice<br>Of a diverted [[motifs/blood\|blood]] and bloody brother. |
| ADAM | But do not so. I have five hundred crowns,<br>The thrifty hire I saved under your father,<br>Which I did store to be my foster-[[characters/nurse\|nurse]]<br>When service should in my old limbs lie lame,<br>And unregarded age in corners thrown.<br>Take that, and he that doth the ravens feed,<br>Yea providently caters for the sparrow,<br>Be comfort to my age. Here is the gold.<br>All this I give you. Let me be your servant.<br>Though I look old, yet I am strong and lusty,<br>For in my youth I never did apply<br>Hot and rebellious liquors in my [[motifs/blood\|blood]],<br>Nor did not with unbashful forehead woo<br>The means of weakness and debility.<br>Therefore my age is as a lusty [[motifs/winter\|winter]],<br>Frosty but kindly. Let me go with you,<br>I’ll do the service of a younger man<br>In all your business and necessities. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | O good old man, how [[motifs/well\|well]] in thee appears<br>The constant service of the antique world,<br>When service sweat for duty, not for meed!<br>Thou art not for the fashion of these times,<br>Where none will sweat but for promotion,<br>And having that do choke their service up<br>Even with the having. It is not so with thee.<br>But, poor old man, thou prun’st a rotten tree,<br>That cannot so much as a blossom yield<br>In lieu of all thy pains and husbandry.<br>But come thy ways. We’ll go along together,<br>And ere we have thy youthful wages spent,<br>We’ll light upon some settled low content. |
| ADAM | Master, go on, and I will follow thee<br>To the last gasp with [[concepts/truth\|truth]] and loyalty.<br>From seventeen years till now almost fourscore<br>Here livèd I, but now live here no more.<br>At seventeen years, many their fortunes seek,<br>But at fourscore, it is too late a week.<br>Yet fortune cannot recompense me better<br>Than to die [[motifs/well\|well]], and not my master’s debtor. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] in man’s clothes as Ganymede; [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena, a shepherdess; and [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O Jupiter, how weary are my spirits! |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | I care not for my spirits, if my legs were not weary. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I could find in my heart to disgrace my man’s apparel and to cry like a woman. But I must comfort the weaker vessel, as doublet and hose ought to show itself courageous to petticoat; therefore, [[concepts/courage\|courage]], good Aliena! |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I pray you, bear with me. I cannot go no further. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | For my part, I had rather bear with you than bear you. Yet I should bear no [[motifs/cross\|cross]] if I did bear you, for I think you have no money in your purse. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], this is the forest of Ardenne. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Ay, now am I in Ardenne; the more [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] I. When I was at [[motifs/home\|home]] I was in a better place; but travellers must be content. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Corin and [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, be so, good [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]]. Look you, who<br>comes here—a young man and an old in solemn talk. |
| CORIN | (to [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]])<br>That is the way to make her scorn you still. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | O Corin, that thou knew’st how I do [[motifs/love\|love]] her! |
| CORIN | I partly guess; for I have loved ere now. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | No, Corin, being old thou canst not guess,<br>Though in thy youth thou wast as true a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]]<br>As ever sighed upon a midnight pillow.<br>But if thy [[motifs/love\|love]] were ever like to mine—<br>As sure I think did never man [[motifs/love\|love]] so—<br>How many actions most ridiculous<br>Hast thou been drawn to by thy fantasy? |
| CORIN | Into a thousand that I have forgotten. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | O, thou didst then never [[motifs/love\|love]] so heartily.<br>If thou rememberest not the slightest folly<br>That ever [[motifs/love\|love]] did make thee run into,<br>Thou hast not loved.<br>Or if thou hast not sat as I do now,<br>Wearing thy hearer in thy mistress’ praise,<br>Thou hast not loved.<br>Or if thou hast not broke from company<br>Abruptly, as my passion now makes me,<br>Thou hast not loved.<br>O, [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]! Exit |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Alas, poor shepherd, searching of thy wound,<br>I have by hard adventure found mine own. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | And I mine. I remember when I was in [[motifs/love\|love]] I broke my sword upon a stone and bid him take that for coming a-[[motifs/night\|night]] to Jane Smile, and I remember the kissing of her batlet, and the cow’s dugs that her pretty chapped hands had milked; and I remember the wooing of a peascod instead of her, from whom I took two cods, and giving her them again, said with weeping [[motifs/tears\|tears]], ‘Wear these for my sake.’ We that are true lovers run into strange capers. But as all is mortal in [[concepts/nature\|nature]], so is all [[concepts/nature\|nature]] in [[motifs/love\|love]] mortal in folly. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Thou speak’st wiser than thou art ware of. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Nay, I shall ne’er be ware of mine own wit till I break my shins against it. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Jove, Jove, this shepherd’s passion<br>Is much upon my fashion. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | And mine, but it grows something stale with me. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I pray you, one of you question yon man<br>If he for gold will give us any food.<br>I faint almost to [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | (to Corin) Holla, you clown! |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Peace, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], he’s not thy kinsman. |
| CORIN | Who calls? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Your betters, sir. |
| CORIN | Else are they very wretched. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]])<br>Peace, I say. (To Corin) Good even to you, friend. |
| CORIN | And to you, gentle sir, and to you all. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I prithee, shepherd, if that [[motifs/love\|love]] or gold<br>Can in this [[motifs/desert\|desert]] place buy entertainment,<br>Bring us where we may rest ourselves, and feed.<br>Here’s a young maid with travel much oppressed,<br>And faints for succour. |
| CORIN | Fair sir, I pity her,<br>And wish, for her sake more than for mine own,<br>My fortunes were more able to relieve her.<br>But I am shepherd to another man,<br>And do not shear the fleeces that I graze.<br>My master is of churlish disposition,<br>And little recks to find the way to heaven<br>By doing deeds of hospitality.<br>Besides, his cot, his flocks, and bounds of feed<br>Are now on sale, and at our sheepcote now<br>By reason of his absence there is nothing<br>That you will feed on. But what is, come see,<br>And in my voice most welcome shall you be. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | What is he that shall buy his flock and pasture? |
| CORIN | That young swain that you saw here but erewhile,<br>That little cares for buying anything. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I pray thee, if it stand with honesty,<br>Buy thou the cottage, pasture, and the flock,<br>And thou shalt have to pay for it of us. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | And we will mend thy wages. I like this place,<br>And willingly could waste my [[motifs/time\|time]] in it. |
| CORIN | Assuredly the thing is to be sold.<br>Go with me. If you like upon report<br>The soil, the profit, and this kind of life,<br>I will your very faithful feeder be,<br>And buy it with your gold right suddenly. Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Amiens, [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]], and other Lords dressed as foresters |
| *(didascalia)* | [AMIENS] (sings)<br>Under the greenwood tree<br>Who loves to lie with me,<br>And turn his merry note<br>Unto the sweet bird’s throat,<br>Come hither, come hither, come hither.<br>Here shall he see<br>No enemy<br>But [[motifs/winter\|winter]] and rough weather. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | More, more, I prithee, more. |
| AMIENS | It will make you [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]], Monsieur [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]]. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I thank it. More, I prithee, more. I can suck [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] out of a song as a weasel sucks eggs. More, I prithee, more. |
| AMIENS | My voice is ragged, I know I cannot please you. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I do not [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you to please me, I do [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you to sing. Come, more; another stanza. Call you ’em stanzas? |
| AMIENS | What you will, Monsieur [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]]. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Nay, I care not for their names, they owe me nothing. Will you sing? |
| AMIENS | More at your request than to please myself. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]] then, if ever I thank any man, I’ll thank you. But that they call compliment is like th’encounter of two dog-apes, and when a man thanks me heartily methinks I have given him a penny and he renders me the beggarly thanks. Come, sing; and you that will not, hold your tongues. |
| AMIENS | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I’ll end the song.—Sirs, cover the while.<br>Lords prepare food and drink<br>The Duke will drink under this tree. (To [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]]) He hath been all this day to look you. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | And I have been all this day to avoid him. He is too disputable for my company. I think of as many matters as he, but I give heaven thanks, and make no boast of them. Come, warble, come. |
| ALL | (sing)<br>Who doth [[concepts/ambition\|ambition]] shun,<br>And loves to live i’th’ sun,<br>Seeking the food he eats<br>And pleased with what he gets,<br>Come hither, come hither, come hither.<br>Here shall he see<br>No enemy<br>But [[motifs/winter\|winter]] and rough weather. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I’ll give you a verse to this note that I made yesterday in despite of my invention. |
| AMIENS | And I’ll sing it. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Thus it goes:<br>If it do come to pass<br>That any man turn ass,<br>Leaving his wealth and ease<br>A stubborn will to please,<br>Ducdame, ducdame, ducdame.<br>Here shall he see<br>Gross fools as he,<br>An if he will come to me. |
| AMIENS | What’s that ‘ducdame’? |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | ’Tis a Greek invocation to call fools into a circle. I’ll go [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] if I can. If I cannot, I’ll rail against all the firstborn of [[settings/egypt\|Egypt]]. |
| AMIENS | And I’ll go seek the Duke; his banquet is prepared. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 2, Scena 6

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and Adam |
| ADAM | Dear master, I can go no further. O, I die for food. Here lie I down and measure out my [[motifs/grave\|grave]]. Farewell, kind master. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Why, how now, Adam? No greater heart in thee? Live a little, comfort a little, cheer thyself a little. If this uncouth forest yield anything savage I will either be food for it or bring it for food to thee. Thy conceit is nearer [[motifs/death\|death]] than thy powers. For my sake be comfortable. Hold [[motifs/death\|death]] awhile at the arm’s end. I will here be with thee presently, and if I bring thee not something to eat, I will give thee leave to die. But if thou diest before I come, thou art a mocker of my labour. [[motifs/well\|Well]] said. Thou lookest cheerly, and I’ll be with thee quickly. Yet thou liest in the bleak air. Come, I will bear thee to some shelter, and thou shalt not die for lack of a dinner if there live anything in this [[motifs/desert\|desert]]. Cheerly, good Adam. [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] carries Adam off |

### Atto 2, Scena 7

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/duke-senior\|Duke Senior]] and Lords dressed as outlaws |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | I think he be transformed into a beast,<br>For I can nowhere find him like a man. |
| FIRST LORD | My lord, he is but even now gone hence.<br>Here was he merry, hearing of a song. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | If he, compact of jars, grow musical<br>We shall have shortly discord in the spheres.<br>Go seek him. Tell him I would speak with him. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] |
| FIRST LORD | He saves my labour by his own approach. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Why, how now, monsieur, what a life is this,<br>That your poor friends must woo your company!<br>What, you look merrily. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | A [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], I met a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] i‘th’ forest,<br>A motley foot—a miserable world!—<br>As I do live by food, I met a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],<br>Who laid him down and basked him in the sun,<br>And railed on Lady Fortune in good terms,<br>In good set terms, and yet a motley [[archetypes/fool\|fool]].<br>‘Good morrow, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],’ quoth I. ‘No, sir,’ quoth he,<br>‘Call me not [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] till heaven hath sent me fortune.’<br>And then he drew a dial from his poke,<br>And looking on it with lack-lustre eye<br>Says very wisely ‘It is ten o‘[[motifs/clock\|clock]].’<br>‘Thus we may see’, quoth he, ‘how the world wags.<br>‘Tis but an hour ago since it was nine,<br>And after one hour more ’twill be eleven.<br>And so from hour to hour we ripe and ripe,<br>And then from hour to hour we rot and rot;<br>And thereby hangs a tale.’ When I did hear<br>The motley [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] thus moral on the [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>My lungs began to crow like chanticleer,<br>That fools should be so deep-contemplative,<br>And I did laugh sans intermission<br>An hour by his dial. O noble [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],<br>A worthy foot—motley’s the only wear. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | What [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] is this? 35 |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | O worthy [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]!—One that hath been a courtier,<br>And says ‘If ladies be but young and fair<br>They have the gift to know it.’ And in his brain,<br>Which is as dry as the remainder biscuit<br>After a voyage, he hath strange places crammed<br>With observation, the which he vents<br>In mangled forms. O that I were a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],<br>I am ambitious for a motley coat. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Thou shalt have one. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | It is my only suit,<br>Provided that you weed your better judgements<br>Of all opinion that grows rank in them<br>That I am wise. I must have liberty<br>Withal, as large a charter as the [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>To blow on whom I please, for so fools have;<br>And they that are most galled with my folly,<br>They most must laugh. And why, sir, must they so?<br>The why is plain as way to parish church:<br>He that a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] doth very wisely hit<br>Doth very foolishly, although he smart,<br>Seem aught but senseless of the bob. If not,<br>The wise man’s folly is anatomized<br>Even by the squandering glances of the [[archetypes/fool\|fool]].<br>Invest me in my motley. Give me leave<br>To speak my mind, and I will through and through<br>Cleanse the foul [[concepts/body\|body]] of th’infected world,<br>If they will patiently receive my medicine. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Fie on thee, I can tell what thou wouldst do. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | What, for a counter, would I do but good? |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Most mischievous foul sin, in chiding sin;<br>For thou thyself hast been a libertine,<br>As sensual as the brutish sting itself,<br>And all th’embossèd sores and headed evils<br>That thou with licence of free foot hast caught<br>Wouldst thou disgorge into the general world. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Why, who cries out on [[concepts/pride\|pride]]<br>That can therein tax any private party?<br>Doth it not flow as hugely as the sea,<br>Till that the weary very means do ebb?<br>What woman in the [[motifs/city\|city]] do I name<br>When that I say the [[motifs/city\|city]]-woman bears<br>The cost of princes on unworthy shoulders?<br>Who can come in and say that I mean her<br>When such a one as she, such is her neighbour?<br>Or what is he of basest function,<br>That says his bravery is not on my cost,<br>Thinking that I mean him, but therein suits<br>His folly to the mettle of my speech?<br>There then, how then, what then, let me see wherein<br>My tongue hath wronged him. If it do him right,<br>Then he hath wronged himself. If he be free,<br>Why then my taxing like a wild goose flies,<br>Unclaimed of any man. But who comes here? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], with sword drawn |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Forbear, and eat no more! |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Why, I have eat none yet. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Nor shalt not till necessity be served. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Of what kind should this cock come of? |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Art thou thus boldened, man, by thy distress?<br>Or else a rude despiser of good manners,<br>That in civility thou seem’st so empty? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | You touched my vein at first. The thorny point<br>Of bare distress hath ta’en from me the show<br>Of smooth civility. Yet am I inland bred,<br>And know some nurture. But forbear, I say.<br>He dies that touches any of this fruit<br>Till I and my affairs are answered. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | An you will not be answered with reason, I must die. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | What would you have? Your gentleness shall force<br>More than your force move us to gentleness. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I almost die for food; and let me have it. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Sit down and feed, and welcome to our table. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Speak you so gently? Pardon me, I pray you.<br>I thought that all things had been savage here,<br>And therefore put I on the countenance<br>Of stern commandment. But whate‘er you are<br>That in this [[motifs/desert\|desert]] inaccessible,<br>Under the shade of [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] boughs,<br>Lose and neglect the creeping hours of [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>If ever you have looked on better days,<br>If ever been where [[motifs/bells\|bells]] have knolled to church,<br>If ever sat at any good man’s feast,<br>If ever from your eyelids wiped a tear,<br>And know what ’tis to pity, and be pitied,<br>Let gentleness my strong enforcement be.<br>In the which [[concepts/hope\|hope]] I blush, and hide my sword. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | True is it that we have seen better days,<br>And have with holy bell been knolled to church,<br>And sat at good men’s feasts, and wiped our eyes<br>Of drops that sacred pity hath engendered.<br>And therefore sit you down in gentleness,<br>And take upon command what help we have<br>That to your wanting may be ministered. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Then but forbear your food a little while<br>Whiles, like a doe, I go to find my fawn<br>And give it food. There is an old poor man<br>Who after me hath many a weary step<br>Limped in pure [[motifs/love\|love]]. Till he be first sufficed,<br>Oppressed with two weak evils, age and hunger,<br>I will not touch a bit. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Go find him out,<br>And we will nothing waste till you return. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I thank ye; and be blessed for your good comfortl Exit |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Thou seest we are not all alone unhappy.<br>This wide and universal theatre<br>Presents more woeful pageants than the scene<br>Wherein we play in. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | All the world’s a stage,<br>And all the men and women merely players.<br>They have their exits and their entrances,<br>And one man in his [[motifs/time\|time]] plays many parts,<br>His acts being seven ages. At first the infant,<br>Mewling and puking in the [[characters/nurse\|nurse]]’s arms.<br>Then the whining schoolboy with his satchel<br>And shining morning face, creeping like snail<br>Unwillingly to school. And then the [[archetypes/lover\|lover]],<br>Sighing like furnace, with a woeful ballad<br>Made to his mistress’ eyebrow. Then, a soldier,<br>Full of strange oaths, and bearded like the pard,<br>Jealous in [[concepts/honour\|honour]], sudden, and quick in quarrel,<br>Seeking the bubble reputation<br>Even in the cannon’s mouth. And then the justice,<br>In fair round belly with good capon lined,<br>With eyes severe and beard of formal cut,<br>Full of wise saws and modern instances;<br>And so he plays his part. The sixth age shifts<br>Into the lean and slippered pantaloon,<br>With spectacles on nose and pouch on side,<br>His youthful hose, [[motifs/well\|well]] saved, a world too wide<br>For his shrunk shank, and his big, manly voice,<br>Turning again toward childish treble, pipes<br>And whistles in his sound. Last scene of all,<br>That ends this strange, eventful [[concepts/history\|history]],<br>Is second childishness and mere oblivion,<br>Sans teeth, sans eyes, sans taste, sans everything. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] bearing Adam |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Welcome. Set down your venerable burden<br>And let him feed. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I thank you most for him. |
| ADAM | So had you need;<br>I scarce can speak to thank you for myself. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Welcome. Fall to. I will not trouble you<br>As yet to question you about your fortunes.<br>Give us some music, and, good cousin, sing.<br>[amiens] (sings)<br>Blow, blow, thou [[motifs/winter\|winter]] [[motifs/wind\|wind]],<br>Thou art not so unkind<br>As man’s ingratitude.<br>Thy tooth is not so keen,<br>Because thou art not seen,<br>Although thy breath be rude.<br>Hey-ho, sing hey-ho, unto the green holly.<br>Most [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]] is feigning, most loving, mere folly.<br>Then hey-ho, the holly;<br>This life is most jolly.<br><br>Freeze, freeze, thou bitter sky,<br>That dost not bite so nigh<br>As benefits forgot.<br>Though thou the waters warp,<br>Thy sting is not so sharp<br>As friend remembered not.<br>Hey-ho, sing hey-ho, unto the green holly.<br>Most [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]] is feigning, most loving, mere folly.<br>Then hey-ho, the holly;<br>This life is most jolly. |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]])<br>If that you were the good Sir Rowland’s son,<br>As you have whispered faithfully you were,<br>And as mine eye doth his effigies witness<br>Most truly limned and living in your face,<br>Be truly welcome hither. I am the Duke<br>That loved your father. The residue of your fortune,<br>Go to my [[motifs/cave\|cave]] and tell me. (To Adam) Good old man,<br>Thou art right welcome, as thy master is.—<br>(To Lords) Support him by the arm. (To [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Give<br>me your hand,<br>And let me all your fortunes understand. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/duke-frederick\|Duke Frederick]], Lords, and [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | Not see him since? Sir, sir, that cannot be.<br>But were I not the better part made [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]],<br>I should not seek an absent argument<br>Of my [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]], thou present. But look to it:<br>Find out thy brother wheresoe’er he is.<br>Seek him with candle. Bring him, dead or living, |
| *(didascalia)* | Within this twelvemonth, or turn thou no more |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | To seek a living in our territory.<br>Thy lands, and all things that thou dost call thine<br>Worth seizure, do we seize into our hands<br>Till thou canst quit thee by thy brother’s mouth<br>Of what we think against thee. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | O that your highness knew my heart in this.<br>I never loved my brother in my life. |
| [[characters/duke-frederick\|DUKE FREDERICK]] | More [[archetypes/villain\|villain]] thou. (To Lords) [[motifs/well\|Well]], push him out of<br>doors,<br>And let my officers of such a [[concepts/nature\|nature]]<br>Make an extent upon his house and lands.<br>Do this expediently, and turn him going. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] with a paper |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Hang there, my verse, in witness of my [[motifs/love\|love]];<br>And thou thrice-crowned [[characters/queen\|queen]] of [[motifs/night\|night]], survey<br>With thy chaste eye, from thy pale sphere above,<br>Thy huntress’ name that my full life doth sway.<br>O [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], these trees shall be my books,<br>And in their barks my thoughts I’ll character<br>That every eye which in this forest looks<br>Shall see thy virtue witnessed everywhere.<br>Run, run, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]; carve on every tree<br>The fair, the chaste, and unexpressive she. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Corin and [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown |
| CORIN | And how like you this shepherd’s life, Master [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]]? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Truly, shepherd, in respect of itself, it is a good life; but in respect that it is a shepherd’s life, it is naught. In respect that it is solitary, I like it very [[motifs/well\|well]]; but in respect that it is private, it is a very vile life. Now in respect it is in the fields, it pleaseth me [[motifs/well\|well]]; but in respect it is not in the [[settings/court\|court]], it is tedious. As it is a spare life, look you, it fits my humour [[motifs/well\|well]]; but as there is no more plenty in it, it goes much against my stomach. Hast any philosophy in thee, shepherd? |
| CORIN | No more but that I know the more one sickens, the worse at ease he is, and that he that wants money, means, and content is without three good friends; that the property of rain is to wet, and [[motifs/fire\|fire]] to burn; that good pasture makes fat sheep; and that a great cause of the [[motifs/night\|night]] is lack of the sun; that he that hath learned no wit by [[concepts/nature\|nature]] nor art may complain of good breeding or comes of a very dull kindred. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Such a one is a natural philosopher. Wast ever in [[settings/court\|court]], shepherd? |
| CORIN | No, truly. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Then thou art damned. |
| CORIN | Nay, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Truly thou art damned, like an ill-roasted egg, all on one side. |
| CORIN | For not being at [[settings/court\|court]]? Your reason? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Why, if thou never wast at [[settings/court\|court]] thou never sawest good manners. If thou never sawest good manners, then thy manners must be wicked, and wickedness is sin, and sin is damnation. Thou art in a parlous state, shepherd. |
| CORIN | Not a whit, [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]]. Those that are good manners at the [[settings/court\|court]] are as ridiculous in the country as the behaviour of the country is most mockable at the [[settings/court\|court]]. You told me you salute not at the [[settings/court\|court]] but you kiss your hands. That courtesy would be uncleanly if courtiers were shepherds. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Instance, briefly; come, instance. |
| CORIN | Why, we are still handling our ewes, and their fells, you know, are greasy. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Why, do not your courtier’s hands sweat? And is not the grease of a mutton as wholesome as the sweat of a man? Shallow, shallow. A better instance, I say. Come. |
| CORIN | Besides, our hands are hard. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Your lips will feel them the sooner. Shallow again. A more sounder instance. Come. |
| CORIN | And they are often tarred over with the surgery of our sheep; and would you have us kiss tar? The courtier’s hands are perfumed with civet. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Most shallow, man. Thou worms’ meat in respect of a good piece of flesh indeed, learn of the wise, and perpend: civet is of a baser birth than tar, the very uncleanly flux of a cat. Mend the instance, shepherd. |
| CORIN | You have too courtly a wit for me. I’ll rest. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Wilt thou rest damned? God help thee, shallow man. God make incision in thee, thou art raw. |
| CORIN | Sir, I am a true labourer. I earn that I eat, get that I wear; owe no man hate, envy no man’s happiness; glad of other men’s good, content with my harm; and the greatest of my [[concepts/pride\|pride]] is to see my ewes graze and my lambs suck. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | That is another simple sin in you, to bring the ewes and the rams together, and to offer to get your living by the copulation of cattle; to be bawd to a bell-wether, and to betray a she-[[motifs/lamb\|lamb]] of a twelvemonth to a crooked-pated old cuckoldly ram, out of all reasonable match. If thou beest not damned for this, the devil himself will have no shepherds. I cannot see else how thou shouldst scape. |
| CORIN | Here comes young Master Ganymede, my new mistress’s brother. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (reads)<br>‘From the east to western Ind<br>No [[motifs/jewel\|jewel]] is like [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>Her worth being mounted on the [[motifs/wind\|wind]]<br>Through all the world bears [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>All the pictures fairest lined<br>Are but black to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>Let no face be kept in mind<br>But the fair of [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].’ |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | I’ll rhyme you so eight years together, dinners, and suppers, and sleeping-hours excepted. It is the right butter-women’s rank to market. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Out, [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | For a taste:<br>If a hart do lack a hind,<br>Let him seek out [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>If the cat will after kind,<br>So, be sure, will [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>Wintered garments must be lined,<br>So must slender [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>They that reap must sheaf and bind,<br>Then to cart with [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>‘Sweetest nut hath sourest rind’,<br>Such a nut is [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>He that sweetest [[motifs/rose\|rose]] will find<br>Must find [[motifs/love\|love]]’s prick, and [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>This is the very false gallop of verses. Why do you infect yourself with them? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Peace, you dull [[archetypes/fool\|fool]], I found them on a tree. |
