| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Truly, the tree yields bad fruit. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I’ll graft it with you, and then I shall graft it with a medlar; then it will be the earliest fruit i’th’ country, for you’ll be rotten ere you be half-ripe, and that’s the right virtue of the medlar. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | You have said; but whether wisely or no, let the forest judge. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/celia\|Celia]], as Aliena, with a writing |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Peace, here comes my sister, reading. Stand aside. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | (reads)<br>‘Why should this a [[motifs/desert\|desert]] be?<br>For it is unpeopled? No.<br>Tongues I’ll hang on every tree,<br>That shall civil sayings show.<br>Some, how brief the life of man<br>Runs his erring pilgrimage,<br>That the stretching of a span<br>Buckles in his sum of age.<br>Some of violated vows<br>’Twixt the souls of friend and friend.<br>But upon the fairest boughs,<br>Or at every sentence end,<br>Will I ‘Rosalinda’ write,<br>Teaching all that read to know<br>The quintessence of every sprite<br>Heaven would in little show.<br>Therefore heaven [[concepts/nature\|nature]] charged<br>That one [[concepts/body\|body]] should be filled<br>With all graces wide-enlarged.<br>[[concepts/nature\|Nature]] presently distilled<br>[[characters/helen\|Helen]]’s cheek, but not her heart,<br>[[characters/cleopatra\|Cleopatra]]’s majesty,<br>Atalanta’s better part,<br>Sad Lucretia’s modesty.<br>Thus [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] of many parts<br>By heavenly synod was devised<br>Of many faces, eyes, and hearts<br>To have the touches dearest prized.<br>Heaven would that she these gifts should have<br>And I to live and die her slave.’ |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O most gentle Jupiter! What tedious homily of [[motifs/love\|love]] have you wearied your parishioners withal, and never cried ‘Have patience, good people.’ |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | How now, back, friends. Shepherd, go off a little. Go with him, sirrah. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Come, shepherd, let us make an honourable retreat, though not with bag and baggage, yet with scrip and scrippage. Exit with Corin |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Didst thou hear these verses? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O yes, I heard them all, and more, too, for some of them had in them more feet than the verses would bear. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | That’s no matter; the feet might bear the verses. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, but the feet were lame, and could not bear themselves without the verse, and therefore stood lamely in the verse. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | But didst thou hear without wondering how thy name should be hanged and carved upon these trees? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I was seven of the nine days out of the [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] before you came; for look here what I found on a palm-tree; (showing [[characters/celia\|Celia]] the verses) I was never so berhymed since Pythagoras’ [[motifs/time\|time]] that I was an Irish rat, which I can hardly remember. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Trow you who hath done this? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Is it a man? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | And a chain that you once wore about his neck. Change you colour? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I prithee, who? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O Lord, Lord, it is a hard matter for friends to meet. But [[settings/mountains\|mountains]] may be removed with earthquakes, and so encounter. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, but who is it? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Is it possible? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, I prithee now with most petitionary vehemence, tell me who it is. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O wonderful, wonderful, and most wonderful-wonderful, and yet again wonderful, and after that out of all whooping! |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Good my complexion! Dost thou think, though I am caparisoned like a man, I have a doublet and hose in my disposition? One inch of delay more is a South Sea of discovery. I prithee tell me who is it quickly, and speak apace. I would thou couldst stammer, that thou mightst pour this concealed man out of thy mouth as wine comes out of a narrow-mouthed bottle—either too much at once, or none at all. I prithee, take the cork out of thy mouth, that I may drink thy tidings. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | So you may put a man in your belly. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Is he of God’s making? What manner of man? Is his head worth a hat? Or his chin worth a beard? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Nay, he hath but a little beard. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why, God will send more, if the man will be thankful. Let me stay the growth of his beard, if thou delay me not the knowledge of his chin. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | It is young [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], that tripped up the wrestler’s heels and your heart both in an instant. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, but the devil take mocking. Speak sad brow and true maid. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I‘[[concepts/faith\|faith]], coz, ’tis he. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Alas the day, what shall I do with my doublet and hose! What did he when thou sawest him? What said he? How looked he? Wherein went he? What makes he here? Did he ask for me? Where remains he? How parted he with thee? And when shalt thou see him again? Answer me in one word. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | You must borrow me Gargantua’s mouth first, ’tis a word too great for any mouth of this age’s size. To say ay and no to these particulars is more than to answer in a catechism. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But doth he know that I am in this forest, and in man’s apparel? Looks he as freshly as he did the day he wrestled? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | It is as easy to count atomies as to resolve the propositions of a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]]; but take a taste of my finding him, and relish it with good observance. I found him under a tree, like a dropped acorn— |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | It may [[motifs/well\|well]] be called Jove’s tree when it drops forth such fruit. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Give me audience, good madam. [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] Proceed. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | There lay he, stretched along like a wounded knight— |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Though it be pity to see such a sight, it [[motifs/well\|well]] becomes the ground. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Cry ‘holla’ to thy tongue, I prithee: it curvets unseasonably.—He was furnished like a hunter— |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O ominous—he comes to kill my heart. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I would sing my song without a burden; thou bringest me out of tune. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Do you not know I am a woman? When I think, I must speak.—Sweet, say on. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | You bring me out. Soft, comes he not here? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | ’Tis he. Slink by, and note him. [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] stand aside |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) I thank you for your company, but, good [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I had as lief have been myself alone. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And so had I. But yet for fashion’ sake, I thank you too for your society. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | God b’wi’you; let’s meet as little as we can. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I do [[motifs/desire\|desire]] we may be better strangers. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I pray you mar no more trees with writing [[motifs/love\|love]]-songs in their barks. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I pray you mar no more of my verses with reading them ill-favouredly. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] is your [[motifs/love\|love]]’s name? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Yes, just. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I do not like her name. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | There was no thought of pleasing you when she was christened. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | What stature is she of? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Just as high as my heart. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | You are full of pretty answers. Have you not been acquainted with goldsmiths’ wives, and conned them out of rings? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Not so; but I answer you right painted cloth, from whence you have studied your questions. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | You have a nimble wit; I think ’twas made of Atalanta’s heels. Will you sit down with me, and we two will rail against our mistress the world, and all our misery? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I will chide no breather in the world but myself, against whom I know most faults. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | The worst fault you have is to be in [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | ’Tis a fault I will not change for your best virtue. I am weary of you. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | By my troth, I was seeking for a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] when I found you. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | He is drowned in the brook. Look but in, and you shall see him. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | There I shall see mine own figure. [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] Which I take to be either a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] or a cipher. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I’ll tarry no longer with you. Farewell, good Signor [[motifs/love\|Love]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I am glad of your departure. Adieu, good Monsieur [[concepts/melancholy\|Melancholy]]. Exit [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/celia\|Celia]]) I will speak to him like a saucy lackey, and under that habit play the knave with him. (To [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) Do you hear, forester? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Very [[motifs/well\|well]]. What would you? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I pray you, what is’t o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | You should ask me what [[motifs/time\|time]] o’ day. There’s no [[motifs/clock\|clock]] in the forest. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Then there is no true [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] in the forest, else sighing every minute and groaning every hour would detect the lazy foot of [[motifs/time\|time]] as [[motifs/well\|well]] as a [[motifs/clock\|clock]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And why not the swift foot of [[motifs/time\|time]]? Had not that been as proper? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | By no means, sir. [[motifs/time\|Time]] travels in divers paces with divers persons. I’ll tell you who [[motifs/time\|time]] ambles withal, who [[motifs/time\|time]] trots withal, who [[motifs/time\|time]] gallops withal, and who he stands still withal. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I prithee, who doth he trot withal? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Marry, he trots hard with a young maid between the contract of her [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]] and the day it is solemnized. If the interim be but a se’nnight, [[motifs/time\|time]]’s pace is so hard that it seems the length of seven year. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Who ambles [[motifs/time\|time]] withal? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With a priest that lacks Latin, and a rich man that hath not the gout; for the one sleeps easily because he cannot study, and the other lives merrily because he feels no [[motifs/pain\|pain]], the one lacking the burden of lean and wasteful learning, the other knowing no burden of heavy tedious penury. These [[motifs/time\|time]] ambles withal. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Who doth he gallop withal? 317 |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With a thief to the gallows; for though he go as softly as foot can fall, he thinks himself too soon there. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Who stays it still withal? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With lawyers in the vacation; for they [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]] between term and term, and then they perceive not how [[motifs/time\|time]] moves. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Where dwell you, pretty youth? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | With this shepherdess, my sister, here in the skirts of the forest, like fringe upon a petticoat. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Are you native of this place? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | As the coney that you see dwell where she is kindled. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Your accent is something finer than you could purchase in so removed a dwelling. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I have been told so of many; but indeed an old religious uncle of mine taught me to speak, who was in his youth an inland man; one that knew courtship too [[motifs/well\|well]], for there he fell in [[motifs/love\|love]]. I have heard him read many lectures against it, and I thank God I am not a woman, to be touched with so many giddy offences as he hath generally taxed their whole sex withal. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Can you remember any of the principal evils that he laid to the charge of women? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | There were none principal; they were all like one another as halfpence are, every one fault seeming monstrous till his fellow-fault came to match it. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I prithee, recount some of them. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | No. I will not cast away my physic but on those that are sick. There is a man haunts the forest that abuses our young plants with carving [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] on their barks; hangs odes upon hawthorns and elegies on brambles; all, forsooth, deifying the name of [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. If I could meet that fancy-monger, I would give him some good counsel, for he seems to have the quotidian of [[motifs/love\|love]] upon him. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I am he that is so [[motifs/love\|love]]-shaked. I pray you, tell me your remedy. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | There is none of my uncle’s marks upon you. He taught me how to know a man in [[motifs/love\|love]], in which cage of rushes I am sure you are not prisoner. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What were his marks? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | A lean cheek, which you have not; a blue eye and sunken, which you have not; an unquestionable spirit, which you have not; a beard neglected, which you have not—but I pardon you for that, for simply your having in beard is a younger brother’s revenue. Then your hose should be ungartered, your bonnet unbanded, your sleeve unbuttoned, your shoe untied, and everything about you demonstrating a careless desolation. But you are no such man. You are rather point-device in your accoutrements, as loving yourself than seeming the [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] of any other. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Fair youth, I would I could make thee believe I [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Me believe it? You may as soon make her that you [[motifs/love\|love]] believe it, which I warrant she is apter to do than to confess she does. That is one of the points in the which women still give the lie to their consciences. But in good sooth, are you he that hangs the verses on the trees wherein [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] is so admired? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I swear to thee, youth, by the white hand of [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], I am that he, that unfortunate he. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But are you so much in [[motifs/love\|love]] as your rhymes speak? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Neither rhyme nor reason can express how much. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[motifs/love\|Love]] is merely a [[concepts/madness\|madness]], and I tell you, deserves as [[motifs/well\|well]] a dark house and a whip as madmen do; and the reason why they are not so punished and cured is that the lunacy is so ordinary that the whippers are in [[motifs/love\|love]] too. Yet I profess curing it by counsel. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Did you ever cure any so? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Yes, one; and in this manner. He was to imagine me his [[motifs/love\|love]], his mistress; and I set him every day to woo me. At which [[motifs/time\|time]] would I, being but a moonish youth, grieve, be effeminate, changeable, [[concepts/longing\|longing]] and liking, proud, fantastical, apish, shallow, inconstant, full of [[motifs/tears\|tears]], full of smiles; for every passion something, and for no passion truly anything, as boys and women are for the most part cattle of this colour—would now like him, now loathe him; then entertain him, then forswear him; now weep for him, then spit at him, that I drave my suitor from his mad humour of [[motifs/love\|love]] to a living humour of [[concepts/madness\|madness]], which was to forswear the full stream of the world and to live in a nook merely monastic. And thus I cured him, and this way will I take upon me to wash your liver as clean as a sound sheep’s heart, that there shall not be one spot of [[motifs/love\|love]] in’t. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I would not be cured, youth. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I would cure you if you would but call me [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] and come every day to my cot, and woo me. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Now by the [[concepts/faith\|faith]] of my [[motifs/love\|love]], I will. Tell me where it is. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Go with me to it, and I’ll show it you. And by the way you shall tell me where in the forest you live. Will you go? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | With all my heart, good youth. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, you must call me [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].—Come, sister. Will you go? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown and Audrey, followed by [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Come apace, good Audrey. I will fetch up your goats, Audrey. And how, Audrey, am I the man yet? Doth my simple feature content you? |
| AUDREY | Your features, Lord warrant us—what features? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | I am here with thee and thy goats as the most capricious poet honest Ovid was among the Goths. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (aside) O knowledge ill-inhabited; worse than Jove in a thatched house. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | When a man’s verses cannot be understood, nor a man’s good wit seconded with the forward [[archetypes/child\|child]], understanding, it strikes a man more dead than a great reckoning in a little room. Truly, I would the gods had made thee poetical. |
| AUDREY | I do not know what ‘poetical’ is. Is it honest in deed and word? Is it a true thing? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | No, truly; for the truest poetry is the most feigning, and lovers are given to poetry; and what they swear in poetry it may be said, as lovers, they do feign. |
| AUDREY | Do you wish, then, that the gods had made me poetical? |
| TOUCHTONE | I do, truly; for thou swearest to me thou art honest. Now if thou wert a poet, I might have some [[concepts/hope\|hope]] thou didst feign. |
| AUDREY | Would you not have me honest? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | No, truly, unless thou wert hard-favoured; for honesty coupled to [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] is to have honey a sauce to sugar. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (aside) A material [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| AUDREY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I am not fair, and therefore I pray the gods make me honest. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Truly, and to cast away honesty upon a foul slut were to put good meat into an unclean dish. |
| AUDREY | I am not a slut, though I thank the gods I am foul. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], praised be the gods for thy foulness. Sluttishness may come hereafter. But be it as it may be, I will marry thee; and to that end I have been with Sir [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] Martext, the vicar of the next village, who hath promised to meet me in this place of the forest, and to couple us. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (aside) I would fain see this meeting. |
| AUDREY | [[motifs/well\|Well]], the gods give us joy. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Amen.—A man may, if he were of a fearful heart, stagger in this attempt; for here we have no temple but the wood, no assembly but horn-beasts. But what though? [[concepts/courage\|Courage]]. As horns are odious, they are necessary. It is said many a man knows no end of his goods. Right: many a man has good horns, and knows no end of them. [[motifs/well\|Well]], that is the dowry of his wife, ‘tis none of his own getting. Horns? Even so. Poor men alone? No, no; the noblest deer hath them as huge as the rascal. Is the single man therefore blessed? No. As a walled town is more worthier than a village, so is the forehead of a married man more honourable than the bare brow of a bachelor. And by how much defence is better than no skill, by so much is a horn more precious than to want. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] Martext |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Here comes Sir [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]].—Sir [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] Martext, you are<br>[[motifs/well\|well]] met. Will you dispatch us here under this tree, or<br>shall we go with you to your chapel? |
| SIR [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] MARTEXT | Is there none here to give the woman? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | I will not take her on gift of any man. |
| SIR [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] MARTEXT | Truly she must be given, or the [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]] is not lawful. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (coming forward) Proceed, proceed. I’ll give her. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Good even, good Monsieur What-ye-call’t. How do you, sir? You are very [[motifs/well\|well]] met. God’ield you for your last company. I am very glad to see you. Even a toy in hand here, sir.<br>[[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] removes his hat<br>Nay, pray be covered. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Will you be married, motley? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | As the ox hath his bow, sir, the [[motifs/horse\|horse]] his curb, and the falcon her [[motifs/bells\|bells]], so man hath his desires; and as pigeons bill, so wedlock would be nibbling. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | And will you, being a man of your breeding, be married under a bush, like a beggar? Get you to church, and have a good priest that can tell you what [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]] is. This fellow will but join you together as they join wainscot; then one of you will prove a shrunk panel and, like green timber, warp, warp. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | I am not in the mind but I were better to be married of him than of another, for he is not like to marry me [[motifs/well\|well]], and not being [[motifs/well\|well]] married, it will be a good excuse for me hereafter to leave my wife. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Go thou with me, and let me counsel thee. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Come, sweet Audrey.<br>We must be married, or we must live in bawdry.<br>Farewell, good Master [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]]. Not<br>O, sweet [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]],<br>O, brave [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]],<br>Leave me not behind thee<br>but |
| *(didascalia)* | [[motifs/wind\|Wind]] away, |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Begone, I say,<br>I will not to wedding with thee. |
| SIR [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] MARTEXT | (aside) ‘Tis no matter. Ne’er a fantastical knave of them all shall flout me out of my calling. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Never talk to me. I will weep. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Do, I prithee, but yet have the grace to consider that [[motifs/tears\|tears]] do not become a man. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But have I not cause to weep? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | As good cause as one would [[motifs/desire\|desire]]; therefore weep. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | His very hair is of the dissembling colour. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Something browner than Judas’s. Marry, his kisses are Judas’s own children. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], his hair is of a good colour. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | An excellent colour. Your chestnut was ever the only colour. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And his kissing is as full of sanctity as the touch of holy bread. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | He hath bought a pair of cast lips of [[characters/diana\|Diana]]. A nun of [[motifs/winter\|winter]]’s sisterhood kisses not more religiously. The very ice of chastity is in them. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But why did he swear he would come this morning, and comes not? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Nay, certainly, there is no [[concepts/truth\|truth]] in him. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Do you think so? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Yes. I think he is not a pick-purse, nor a [[motifs/horse\|horse]]-stealer; but for his verity in [[motifs/love\|love]], I do think him as concave as a covered goblet, or a worm-eaten nut. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Not true in [[motifs/love\|love]]? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Yes, when he is in. But I think he is not in. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | You have heard him swear downright he was. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | ‘Was’ is not ‘is’. Besides, the oath of a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] is no stronger than the word of a tapster. They are both the confirmer of false reckonings. He attends here in the forest on the Duke your father. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I met the Duke yesterday, and had much question with him. He asked me of what parentage I was. I told him, of as good as he, so he laughed and let me go. But what talk we of fathers when there is such a man as [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O that’s a brave man. He writes brave verses, speaks brave words, swears brave oaths, and breaks them bravely, quite traverse, athwart the heart of his [[archetypes/lover\|lover]], as a puny tilter that spurs his [[motifs/horse\|horse]] but on one side breaks his staff, like a noble goose. But all’s brave that youth mounts, and folly guides. Who comes here? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Corin |
| CORIN | Mistress and master, you have oft enquired<br>After the shepherd that complained of [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>Who you saw sitting by me on the turf,<br>Praising the proud disdainful shepherdess<br>That was his mistress. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], and what of him? |
| CORIN | If you will see a pageant truly played<br>Between the pale complexion of true [[motifs/love\|love]]<br>And the red glow of scorn and proud disdain,<br>Go hence a little, and I shall conduct you,<br>If you will mark it. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/celia\|Celia]]) O come, let us remove.<br>The sight of lovers feedeth those in [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>(To Corin) Bring us to this sight, and you shall say<br>I’ll prove a busy actor in their play. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] and [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Sweet [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], do not scorn me, do not, [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]].<br>Say that you [[motifs/love\|love]] me not, but say not so<br>In bitterness. The common executioner,<br>Whose heart th’accustomed sight of [[motifs/death\|death]] makes<br>hard,<br>Falls not the axe upon the humbled neck<br>But first begs pardon. Will you sterner be<br>Than he that dies and lives by bloody drops? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede, [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena, and Corin, and stand aside |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | (to [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]])<br>I would not be thy executioner.<br>I fly thee for I would not injure thee.<br>Thou tell‘st me there is murder in mine eye.<br>’Tis pretty, sure, and very probable<br>That eyes, that are the frail’st and softest things,<br>Who shut their coward gates on atomies,<br>Should be called tyrants, butchers, murderers.<br>Now I do frown on thee with all my heart,<br>And if mine eyes can wound, now let them kill thee.<br>Now counterfeit to swoon, why now fall down;<br>Or if thou canst not, O, for shame, for shame,<br>Lie not, to say mine eyes are murderers.<br>Now show the wound mine eye hath made in thee.<br>Scratch thee but with a pin, and there remains<br>Some scar of it. Lean upon a rush,<br>The cicatrice and capable impressure<br>Thy palm some moment keeps. But now mine eyes,<br>Which I have darted at thee, hurt thee not;<br>Nor I am sure there is no force in eyes<br>That can do hurt. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | O dear [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]],<br>If ever—as that ever may be near—<br>You meet in some fresh cheek the power of fancy,<br>Then shall you know the wounds invisible<br>That [[motifs/love\|love]]’s keen arrows make. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | But till that [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>Come not thou near me. And when that [[motifs/time\|time]] comes,<br>Afflict me with thy mocks, pity me not,<br>As till that [[motifs/time\|time]] I shall not pity thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (coming forward)<br>And why, I pray you? Who might be your mother,<br>That you insult, exult, and all at once,<br>Over the wretched? What though you have no<br>[[concepts/beauty\|beauty]]—<br>As, by my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I see no more in you<br>Than without candle may go dark to bed—<br>Must you be therefore proud and pitiless?<br>Why, what means this? Why do you look on me?<br>I see no more in you than in the ordinary<br>Of [[concepts/nature\|nature]]’s sale-work.—‘Od’s my little life,<br><br>I think she means to tangle my eyes, too.<br>No, [[concepts/faith\|faith]], proud mistress, [[concepts/hope\|hope]] not after it.<br>’Tis not your inky brows, your black silk hair,<br>Your bugle eyeballs, nor your cheek of cream,<br>That can entame my spirits to your worship.<br>(To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) You, foolish shepherd, wherefore do you<br>follow her<br>Like foggy south, puffing with [[motifs/wind\|wind]] and rain?<br>You are a thousand times a properer man<br>Than she a woman. ‘Tis such fools as you<br>That makes the world full of ill-favoured children.<br>’Tis not her glass but you that flatters her,<br>And out of you she sees herself more proper<br>Than any of her lineaments can show her.<br>(To [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) But, mistress, know yourself; down on<br>your knees<br>And thank heaven, fasting, for a good man’s [[motifs/love\|love]];<br>For I must tell you friendly in your ear,<br>Sell when you can. You are not for all markets.<br>Cry the man [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]], [[motifs/love\|love]] him, take his offer;<br>Foul is most foul, being foul to be a scoffer.—<br>So, take her to thee, shepherd. Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Sweet youth, I pray you chide a year together.<br>I had rather hear you chide than this man woo. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) He’s fallen in [[motifs/love\|love]] with your foulness, (to [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) and she’ll fall in [[motifs/love\|love]] with my anger. If it be so, as fast as she answers thee with frowning looks, I’ll sauce her with bitter words. (To [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) Why look you so upon me? |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | For no ill will I bear you. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I pray you do not fall in [[motifs/love\|love]] with me,<br>For I am falser than vows made in wine.<br>Besides, I like you not. If you will know my house,<br>’Tis at the tuft of olives, here hard by.<br>(To [[characters/celia\|Celia]]) Will you go, sister? (To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) Shepherd,<br>ply her hard.—<br>Come, sister. (To [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) Shepherdess, look on him<br>better,<br>And be not proud. Though all the world could see,<br>None could be so abused in sight as he.—<br>Come, to our flock. Exeunt [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], [[characters/celia\|Celia]], and Corin |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | (aside)<br>Dead shepherd, now I find thy saw of might:<br>‘Who ever loved that loved not at first sight?’ |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Sweet [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]— |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Ha, what sayst thou, [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Sweet [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], pity me. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Why, I am sorry for thee, gentle [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Wherever sorrow is, relief would be.<br>If you do sorrow at my grief in [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>By giving [[motifs/love\|love]] your sorrow and my grief<br>Were both extermined. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Thou hast my [[motifs/love\|love]], is not that neighbourly? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | I would have you. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Why, that were covetousness.<br>[[characters/silvius\|Silvius]], the [[motifs/time\|time]] was that I hated thee;<br>And yet it is not that I bear thee [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>But since that thou canst talk of [[motifs/love\|love]] so [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>Thy company, which erst was irksome to me,<br>I will endure; and I’ll employ thee, too.<br>But do not look for further recompense<br>Than thine own gladness that thou art employed. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | So holy and so perfect is my [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>And I in such a [[concepts/poverty\|poverty]] of grace,<br>That I shall think it a most plenteous crop<br>To glean the broken ears after the man<br>That the main harvest reaps. Loose now and then<br>A scattered smile, and that I’ll live upon. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Know’st thou the youth that spoke to me erewhile? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Not very [[motifs/well\|well]], but I have met him oft,<br>And he hath bought the cottage and the bounds<br>That the old Carlot once was master of. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Think not I [[motifs/love\|love]] him, though I ask for him.<br>‘Tis but a peevish boy. Yet he talks [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>But what care I for words? Yet words do [[motifs/well\|well]]<br>When he that speaks them pleases those that hear.<br>It is a pretty youth—not very pretty—<br>But sure he’s proud; and yet his [[concepts/pride\|pride]] becomes him.<br>He’ll make a proper man. The best thing in him<br>Is his complexion; and faster than his tongue<br>Did make offence, his eye did heal it up.<br>He is not very tall; yet for his years he’s tall.<br>His leg is but so-so; and yet ’tis [[motifs/well\|well]].<br>There was a pretty redness in his lip,<br>A little riper and more lusty-red<br>Than that mixed in his cheek. ’Twas just the<br>difference<br>Betwixt the constant red and mingled damask.<br>There be some women, [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]], had they marked him<br>In parcels as I did, would have gone near<br>To fall in [[motifs/love\|love]] with him; but for my part,<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] him not, nor hate him not. And yet<br>Have I more cause to hate him than to [[motifs/love\|love]] him,<br>For what had he to do to chide at me?<br>He said mine eyes were black, and my hair black,<br>And now I am remembered, scorned at me.<br>I marvel why I answered not again.<br>But that’s all one. Omittance is no quittance.<br>I’ll write to him a very taunting letter,<br>And thou shalt bear it. Wilt thou, [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], with all my heart. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | I’ll write it straight.<br>The matter’s in my head and in my heart.<br>I will be bitter with him, and passing short.<br>Go with me, [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede, [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena, and [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I prithee, pretty youth, let me be better acquainted with thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | They say you are a [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] fellow. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I am so. I do [[motifs/love\|love]] it better than laughing. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Those that are in extremity of either are abominable fellows, and betray themselves to every modern censure worse than drunkards. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Why, ’tis good to be sad and say nothing. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why then, ’tis good to be a post. 9 |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | I have neither the scholar’s [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]], which is emulation, nor the musician‘s, which is fantastical, nor the courtier’s, which is proud, nor the soldier‘s, which is ambitious, nor the lawyer’s, which is politic, nor the lady‘s, which is nice, nor the [[archetypes/lover\|lover]]’s, which is all these; but it is a [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]] of mine own, compounded of many simples, extracted from many objects, and indeed the sundry contemplation of my travels, in which my often rumination wraps me in a most humorous sadness. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | A traveller! By my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], you have great reason to be sad. I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] you have sold your own lands to see other men’s. Then to have seen much and to have nothing is to have rich eyes and poor hands. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Yes, I have gained my experience. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And your experience makes you sad. I had rather have a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] to make me merry than experience to make me sad—and to travel for it too! |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Good day and happiness, dear [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Nay then, God b’wi’you an you talk in [[forms/blank-verse\|blank verse]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] Farewell, Monsieur Traveller. Look you lisp, and wear strange suits; disable all the benefits of your own country; be out of [[motifs/love\|love]] with your nativity, and almost chide God for making you that countenance you are, or I will scarce think you have swam in a gondola. Exit [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] Why, how now, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]? Where have you been all this while? You a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]]? An you serve me such another trick, never come in my sight more. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | My fair [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], I come within an hour of my promise. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]]. | Break an hour’s promise in [[motifs/love\|love]]! He that will divide a minute into a thousand parts and break but a part of the thousand part of a minute in the affairs of [[motifs/love\|love]], it may be said of him that Cupid hath clapped him o’th’ shoulder, but I’ll warrant him heartwhole. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Pardon me, dear [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. 47 |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, an you be so tardy, come no more in my sight. I had as lief be wooed of a snail. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Of a snail? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, of a snail; for though he comes slowly, he carries his house on his head—a better jointure, I think, than you make a woman. Besides, he brings his destiny with him. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What’s that? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why, horns, which such as you are fain to be beholden to your wives for. But he comes armed in his fortune, and prevents the slander of his wife. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Virtue is no hornmaker, and my [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] is virtuous. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And I am your [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | It pleases him to call you so; but he hath a [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] of a better leer than you. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Come, woo me, woo me, for now I am in a holiday humour, and like enough to consent. What would you say to me now an I were your very, very [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I would kiss before I spoke. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, you were better speak first, and when you were gravelled for lack of matter you might take occasion to kiss. Very good orators, when they are out, they will spit; and for lovers, lacking—God warr’nt us—matter, the cleanliest shift is to kiss. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | How if the kiss be denied? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Then she puts you to entreaty, and there begins new matter. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Who could be out, being before his beloved mistress? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Marry, that should you if I were your mistress, or I should think my honesty ranker than my wit. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What, of my suit? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Not out of your apparel, and yet out of your suit. Am not I your [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I take some joy to say you are because I would be talking of her. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], in her person I say I will not have you. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Then in mine own person I die. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | No, [[concepts/faith\|faith]]; die by attorney. The poor world is almost six thousand years old, and in all this [[motifs/time\|time]] there was not any man died in his own person, videlicet, in a [[motifs/love\|love]]-cause. [[characters/troilus\|Troilus]] had his brains dashed out with a Grecian club, yet he did what he could to die before, and he is one of the patterns of [[motifs/love\|love]]. Leander, he would have lived many a fair year though [[archetypes/hero\|Hero]] had turned nun if it had not been for a hot midsummer [[motifs/night\|night]], for, good youth, he went but forth to wash him in the Hellespont and, being taken with the cramp, was drowned; and the foolish chroniclers of that age found it was [[archetypes/hero\|Hero]] of Sestos. But these are all lies. Men have died from [[motifs/time\|time]] to [[motifs/time\|time]], and worms have eaten them, but not for [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I would not have my right [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] of this mind, for I protest her frown might kill me. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | By this hand, it will not kill a fly. But come, now I will be your [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] in a more coming-on disposition; and ask me what you will, I will grant it. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Then [[motifs/love\|love]] me, [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Yes, [[concepts/faith\|faith]], will I, Fridays and Saturdays and all. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And wilt thou have me? no |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, and twenty such. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | What sayst thou? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Are you not good? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] so. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why then, can one [[motifs/desire\|desire]] too much of a good thing? (To [[characters/celia\|Celia]]) Come, sister, you shall be the priest and marry us.—Give me your hand, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]].—What do you say, sister? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | (to [[characters/celia\|Celia]]) Pray thee, marry us. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I cannot say the words. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | You must begin, ‘Will you, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]’— |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Go to. Will you, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], have to wife this [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I Will. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, but when? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Why now, as fast as she can marry us. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Then you must say, ‘I take thee, [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], for wife.’ |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I take thee, [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], for wife. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I might ask you for your commission; but I do take thee, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], for my husband. There’s a girl goes before the priest; and certainly a woman’s thought runs before her actions. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | So do all thoughts; they are winged. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Now tell me how long you would have her after you have possessed her? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | For ever and a day. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Say a day without the ever. No, no, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]; men are April when they woo, December when they wed. Maids are May when they are maids, but the sky changes when they are wives. I will be more jealous of thee than a Barbary cock-pigeon over his hen, more clamorous than a parrot against rain, more newfangled than an ape, more giddy in my desires than a monkey. I will weep for nothing, like [[characters/diana\|Diana]] in the fountain, and I will do that when you are disposed to be merry. I will laugh like a hyena, and that when thou art inclined to [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | But will my [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] do so? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | By my life, she will do as I do. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | O, but she is wise. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Or else she could not have the wit to do this. The wiser, the waywarder. Make the doors upon a woman’s wit, and it will out at the casement. Shut that, and ‘twill out at the key-hole. Stop that, ’twill fly with the smoke out at the chimney. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | A man that had a wife with such a wit, he might say ‘Wit, whither wilt?’ |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Nay, you might keep that check for it till you met your wife’s wit going to your neighbour’s bed. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And what wit could wit have to excuse that? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Marry, to say she came to seek you there. You shall never take her without her answer unless you take her without her tongue. O, that woman that cannot make her fault her husband’s occasion, let her never [[characters/nurse\|nurse]] her [[archetypes/child\|child]] herself, for she will breed it like a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | For these two hours, [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], I will leave thee. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Alas, dear [[motifs/love\|love]], I cannot lack thee two hours. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I must attend the Duke at dinner. By two o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]] I will be with thee again. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Ay, go your ways, go your ways. I knew what you would prove; my friends told me as much, and I thought no less. That flattering tongue of yours won me. ‘Tis but one cast away, and so, come, [[motifs/death\|death]]! Two o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]] is your hour? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Ay, sweet [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | By my troth, and in good earnest, and so God mend me, and by all pretty oaths that are not dangerous, if you break one jot of your promise or come one minute behind your hour, I will think you the most pathetical break-promise, and the most hollow [[archetypes/lover\|lover]], and the most unworthy of her you call [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] that may be chosen out of the gross band of the unfaithful. Therefore beware my censure, and keep your promise. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | With no less religion than if thou wert indeed my [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. So, adieu. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[motifs/time\|Time]] is the old justice that examines all such offenders; and let [[motifs/time\|Time]] try. Adieu. Exit [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | You have simply misused our sex in your [[motifs/love\|love]]-prate. We must have your doublet and hose plucked over your head, and show the world what the bird hath done to her own nest. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O coz, coz, coz, my pretty little coz, that thou didst know how many fathom deep I am in [[motifs/love\|love]]. But it cannot be sounded. My affection hath an unknown [[characters/bottom\|bottom]], like the Bay of Portugal. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Or rather bottomless, that as fast as you pour affection in, it runs out. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | No, that same wicked bastard of [[characters/venus\|Venus]], that was begot of thought, conceived of spleen, and born of [[concepts/madness\|madness]], that blind rascally boy that abuses everyone’s eyes because his own are out, let him be judge how deep I am in [[motifs/love\|love]]. I’ll tell thee, Aliena, I cannot be out of the sight of [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]. I’ll go find a [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] and sigh till he come. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | And I’ll [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]] and Lords dressed as foresters [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] Which is he that killed the deer? FIRST LORD Sir, it was I. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | (to the others) Let’s present him to the Duke like a Roman conqueror. And it would do [[motifs/well\|well]] to set the deer’s horns upon his head for a branch of victory. Have you no song, forester, for this purpose? |
| SECOND LORD | Yes, sir. |
| [[characters/jaques\|JAQUES]] | Sing it. ’Tis no matter how it be in tune, so it make noise enough. |
| LORDS | (sing)<br>What shall he have that killed the deer?<br>His leather skin and horns to wear.<br>Then sing him [[motifs/home\|home]]; the rest shall bear<br>This burden.<br>Take thou no scorn to wear the horn;<br>It was a crest ere thou wast born.<br>Thy father’s father wore it,<br>And thy father bore it.<br>The horn, the horn, the lusty horn<br>Is not a thing to laugh to scorn. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | How say you now? Is it not past two o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]]? And here much [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I warrant you, with pure [[motifs/love\|love]] and troubled brain he hath ta’en his bow and arrows and is gone forth to [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Look who comes here. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | (to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]])<br>My errand is to you, fair youth.<br>My gentle [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] did bid me give you this.<br>He offers [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] a letter, which she takes and reads<br>I know not the contents, but as I guess<br>By the stern brow and waspish action<br>Which she did use as she was writing of it,<br>It bears an angry tenor. Pardon me;<br>I am but as a guiltless messenger. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Patience herself would startle at this letter,<br>And play the swaggerer. Bear this, bear all.<br>She says I am not fair, that I lack manners;<br>She calls me proud, and that she could not [[motifs/love\|love]] me<br>Were man as rare as Phoenix. ‘Od’s my will,<br>Her [[motifs/love\|love]] is not the hare that I do hunt.<br>Why writes she so to me? [[motifs/well\|Well]], shepherd, [[motifs/well\|well]],<br>This is a letter of your own device. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | No, I protest; I know not the contents.<br>[[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] did write it. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Come, come, you are a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]],<br>And turned into the extremity of [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>I saw her hand. She has a leathern hand,<br>A free-stone coloured hand. I verily did think<br>That her old gloves were on; but ’twas her hands.<br>She has a housewife’s hand—but that’s no matter.<br>I say she never did invent this letter.<br>This is a man’s invention, and his hand. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Sure, it is hers. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why, ’tis a boisterous and a cruel style,<br>A style for challengers. Why, she defies me,<br>Like Turk to Christian. Women’s gentle brain<br>Could not drop forth such giant-rude invention,<br>Such Ethiop words, blacker in their effect<br>Than in their countenance. Will you hear the letter? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | So please you, for I never heard it yet,<br>Yet heard too much of [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]’s cruelty. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | She Phoebes me. Mark how the [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] writes:<br>(reads) ‘Art thou god to shepherd turned,<br>That a maiden’s heart hath burned?’<br>Can a woman rail thus? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Call you this railing? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (reads)<br>‘Why, thy godhead laid apart,<br>Warr’st thou with a woman’s heart?’<br>Did you ever hear such railing?<br>‘Whiles the eye of man did woo me<br>That could do no vengeance to me.’—<br>Meaning me a beast.<br>‘If the scorn of your bright eyne<br>Have power to raise such [[motifs/love\|love]] in mine,<br>Alack, in me what strange effect<br>Would they work in mild aspect?<br>Whiles you chid me I did [[motifs/love\|love]];<br>How then might your prayers move?<br>He that brings this [[motifs/love\|love]] to thee<br>Little knows this [[motifs/love\|love]] in me,<br>And by him seal up thy mind<br>Whether that thy youth and kind<br>Will the faithful offer take<br>Of me, and all that I can make,<br>Or else by him my [[motifs/love\|love]] deny,<br>And then I’ll study how to die.’ |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Call you this chiding? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Alas, poor shepherd. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Do you pity him? No, he deserves no pity. (To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) Wilt thou [[motifs/love\|love]] such a woman? What, to make thee an instrument, and play false strains upon thee?—not to be endured. [[motifs/well\|Well]], go your way to her—for I see [[motifs/love\|love]] hath made thee a tame snake—and say this to her: that if she [[motifs/love\|love]] me, I charge her to [[motifs/love\|love]] thee. If she will not, I will never have her unless thou entreat for her. If you be a true [[archetypes/lover\|lover]], hence, and not a word; for here comes more company. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Good morrow, fair ones. Pray you, if you know,<br>Where in the purlieus of this forest stands<br>A sheepcote fenced about with olive trees? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | West of this place, down in the neighbour [[characters/bottom\|bottom]].<br>The rank of osiers by the murmuring stream<br>Left on your right hand brings you to the place.<br>But at this hour the house doth keep itself.<br>There’s none within. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | If that an eye may profit by a tongue,<br>Then should I know you by description.<br>Such garments, and such years. ‘The boy is fair,<br>Of female favour, and bestows himself<br>Like a ripe sister. The woman low<br>And browner than her brother.’ Are not you<br>The owner of the house I did enquire for? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | It is no boast, being asked, to say we are. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] doth commend him to you both,<br>And to that youth he calls his [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]<br>He sends this bloody napkin. Are you he? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I am. What must we understand by this? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Some of my shame, if you will know of me<br>What man I am, and how, and why, and where<br>This handkerchief was stained. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | I pray you tell it. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | When last the young [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] parted from you,<br>He left a promise to return again |
| *(didascalia)* | Within an hour, and pacing through the forest, |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Chewing the food of sweet and bitter fancy,<br>Lo what befell. He threw his eye aside,<br>And mark what object did present itself.<br>Under an old oak, whose boughs were mossed with age<br>And high top bald with dry antiquity,<br>A wretched, ragged man, o‘ergrown with hair,<br>Lay sleeping on his back. About his neck<br>A green and gilded snake had wreathed itself,<br>Who with her head, nimble in threats, approached<br>The opening of his mouth. But suddenly<br>Seeing [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], it unlinked itself,<br>And with indented glides did slip away<br>Into a bush, under which bush’s shade<br>A lioness, with udders all drawn dry,<br>Lay couching, head on ground, with catlike watch<br>When that the sleeping man should stir. For ’tis<br>The royal disposition of that beast<br>To prey on nothing that doth seem as dead.<br>This seen, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] did approach the man<br>And found it was his brother, his elder brother. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | O, I have heard him speak of that same brother,<br>And he did render him the most unnatural<br>That lived amongst men. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | And [[motifs/well\|well]] he might so do,<br>For [[motifs/well\|well]] I know he was unnatural. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]. Did he leave him there,<br>Food to the sucked and hungry lioness? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Twice did he turn his back, and purposed so.<br>But kindness, nobler ever than [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]],<br>And [[concepts/nature\|nature]], stronger than his just occasion,<br>Made him give battle to the lioness,<br>Who quickly fell before him; in which hurtling<br>From miserable slumber I awaked. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Are you his brother? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Was’t you he rescued? |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Was’t you that did so oft contrive to kill him? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | ‘Twas I, but ’tis not I. I do not shame<br>To tell you what I was, since my [[concepts/conversion\|conversion]]<br>So sweetly tastes, being the thing I am. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But for the bloody napkin? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | By and by.<br>When from the first to last betwixt us two<br>[[motifs/tears\|Tears]] our recountments had most kindly bathed—<br>As how I came into that [[motifs/desert\|desert]] place—<br>I’ brief, he led me to the gentle Duke,<br>Who gave me fresh array, and entertainment,<br>Committing me unto my brother’s [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>Who led me instantly unto his [[motifs/cave\|cave]],<br>There stripped himself, and here upon his arm<br>The lioness had torn some flesh away,<br>Which all this while had bled. And now he fainted,<br>And cried in fainting upon [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>Brief, I recovered him, bound up his wound,<br>And after some small space, being strong at heart,<br>He sent me hither, stranger as I am,<br>To tell this story, that you might excuse<br>His broken promise, and to give this napkin,<br>Dyed in his [[motifs/blood\|blood]], unto the shepherd youth<br>That he in sport doth call his [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]].<br>[[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] faints |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Why, how now, Ganymede, sweet Ganymede! |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Many will swoon when they do look on [[motifs/blood\|blood]]. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | There is more in it. Cousin Ganymede! |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Look, he recovers. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I would I were at [[motifs/home\|home]]. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | We’ll lead you thither.<br>(To [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]]) I pray you, will you take him by the arm? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Be of good cheer, youth. You a man? You lack a man’s heart. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I do so, I confess it. Ah, sirrah, a [[concepts/body\|body]] would think this was [[motifs/well\|well]] counterfeited. I pray you, tell your brother how [[motifs/well\|well]] I counterfeited. Heigh-ho! |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | This was not counterfeit. There is too great testimony in your complexion that it was a passion of earnest. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Counterfeit, I assure you. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]] then, take a good heart, and counterfeit to be a man. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | So I do; but, i’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], I should have been a woman by right. |
| [[characters/celia\|CELIA]] | Come, you look paler and paler. Pray you, draw homewards. Good sir, go with us. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | That will I, for I must bear answer back<br>How you excuse my brother, [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I shall devise something. But I pray you commend my counterfeiting to him. Will you go? |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown and Audrey |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | We shall find a [[motifs/time\|time]], Audrey. Patience, gentle Audrey. |
| AUDREY | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], the priest was good enough, for all the old gentleman’s saying. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | A most wicked Sir [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]], Audrey, a most vile Martext. But, Audrey, there is a youth here in the forest lays claim to you. |
| AUDREY | Ay, I know who ’tis. He hath no interest in me in the world. Here comes the man you mean. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter William |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | It is meat and drink to me to see a clown. By my troth, we that have good wits have much to answer for. We shall be flouting; we cannot hold. |
| WILLIAM | Good ev’n, Audrey. |
| AUDREY | God ye good ev’n, William. |
| WILLIAM | (to [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]]) And good ev’n to you, sir. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Good ev’n, gentle friend. Cover thy head, cover thy head. Nay, prithee, be covered. How old are you, friend? |
| WILLIAM | Five-and-twenty, sir. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | A ripe age. Is thy name William? |
| WILLIAM | William, sir. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | A fair name. Wast born i’th’ forest here? |
| WILLIAM | Ay, sir, I thank God. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Thank God—a good answer. Art rich? |
| WILLIAM | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], sir, so-so. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | So-so is good, very good, very excellent good. And yet it is not, it is but so-so. Art thou wise? |
| WILLIAM | Ay, sir, I have a pretty wit. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Why, thou sayst [[motifs/well\|well]]. I do now remember a saying: ‘The [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] doth think he is wise, but the wise man knows himself to be a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]].’ The heathen philosopher, when he had a [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to eat a grape, would open his lips when he put it into his mouth, meaning thereby that grapes were made to eat, and lips to open. You do [[motifs/love\|love]] this maid? |
| WILLIAM | I do, sir. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Give me your hand. Art thou learned? |
| WILLIAM | No, sir. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Then learn this of me: to have is to have. For it is a figure in rhetoric that drink, being poured out of a cup into a glass, by filling the one doth empty the other. For all your writers do consent that ipse is he. Now you are not ipse, for I am he. |
| WILLIAM | Which he, sir? |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | He, sir, that must marry this woman. Therefore, you clown, abandon—which is in the vulgar, leave—the society—which in the boorish is company—of this femate—which in the common is woman; which together is, abandon the society of this female, or, clown, thou perishest; or, to thy better understanding, diest; or, to wit, I kill thee, make thee away, translate thy life into [[motifs/death\|death]], thy liberty into bondage. I will deal in [[motifs/poison\|poison]] with thee, or in bastinado, or in steel. I will bandy with thee in faction, I will o’errun thee with policy. I will kill thee a hundred and fifty ways. Therefore tremble, and depart. |
| AUDREY | Do, good William. |
| WILLIAM | God rest you merry, sir. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Corin |
| CORIN | Our master and mistress seeks you. Come, away, away. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Trip, Audrey, trip, Audrey. (To Corin) I attend, I attend. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 5, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] and [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]] |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Is’t possible that on so little acquaintance you should like her? That but seeing, you should [[motifs/love\|love]] her? And loving, woo? And wooing, she should grant? And will you persevere to enjoy her? |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | Neither call the giddiness of it in question, the [[concepts/poverty\|poverty]] of her, the small acquaintance, my sudden wooing, nor her sudden consenting; but say with me, ‘I [[motifs/love\|love]] Aliena’; say with her, that she loves me; consent with both that we may enjoy each other. It shall be to your good, for my father’s house and all the revenue that was old Sir Rowland’s will I estate upon you, and here live and die a shepherd. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | You have my consent. Let your wedding be tomorrow. Thither will I invite the Duke and all’s contented followers. Go you, and prepare Aliena; for look you, here comes my [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | God save you, brother. |
| [[characters/oliver\|OLIVER]] | And you, fair sister. Exit |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O, my dear [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], how it grieves me to see thee wear thy heart in a scarf. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | It is my arm. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I thought thy heart had been wounded with the claws of a [[motifs/lion\|lion]]. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Wounded it is, but with the eyes of a lady. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Did your brother tell you how I counterfeited to swoon when he showed me your handkerchief? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Ay, and greater wonders than that. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | O, I know where you are. Nay, ‘tis true. There was never anything so sudden but the fight of two rams, and Caesar’s thrasonical brag of ‘I came, saw, and overcame’, for your brother and my sister no sooner met but they looked; no sooner looked but they loved; no sooner loved but they sighed; no sooner sighed but they asked one another the reason; no sooner knew the reason but they sought the remedy; and in these degrees have they made a pair of stairs to [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]], which they will climb incontinent, or else be incontinent before [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]]. They are in the very wrath of [[motifs/love\|love]], and they will together. Clubs cannot part them. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | They shall be married tomorrow, and I will bid the Duke to the nuptial. But O, how bitter a thing it is to look into happiness through another man’s eyes. By so much the more shall I tomorrow be at the height of heart-heaviness by how much I shall think my brother happy in having what he wishes for. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why, then, tomorrow I cannot serve your turn for [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I can live no longer by thinking. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I will weary you then no longer with idle talking. Know of me then—for now I speak to some purpose—that I know you are a gentleman of good conceit. I speak not this that you should bear a good opinion of my knowledge, insomuch I say I know you are; neither do I labour for a greater esteem than may in some little measure draw a belief from you to do yourself good, and not to grace me. Believe then, if you please, that I can do strange things. I have since I was three year old conversed with a magician, most profound in his art, and yet not damnable. If you do [[motifs/love\|love]] [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] so near the heart as your gesture cries it out, when your brother marries Aliena shall you marry her. I know into what straits of fortune she is driven, and it is not impossible to me, if it appear not inconvenient to you, to set her before your eyes tomorrow, human as she is, and without any danger. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Speakest thou in sober meanings? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | By my life, I do, which I tender dearly, though I say I am a magician. Therefore put you in your best array, bid your friends: for if you will be married tomorrow, you shall; and to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] if you will. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] and [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Look, here comes a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] of mine and a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] of hers. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | (to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]])<br>Youth, you have done me much ungentleness,<br>To show the letter that I writ to you. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | I care not if I have. It is my study<br>To seem despiteful and ungentle to you.<br>You are there followed by a faithful shepherd.<br>Look upon him; [[motifs/love\|love]] him. He worships you. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | (to [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]])<br>Good shepherd, tell this youth what ’tis to [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | It is to be all made of sighs and [[motifs/tears\|tears]],<br>And so am I for [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | And I for Ganymede. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And I for [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And I for no woman. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | It is to be all made of [[concepts/faith\|faith]] and service,<br>And so am I for [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | And I for Ganymede. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And I for [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And I for no woman. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | It is to be all made of fantasy,<br>All made of passion, and all made of wishes,<br>All adoration, duty, and observance,<br>All humbleness, all patience and impatience,<br>All purity, all trial, all obedience,<br>And so am I for [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | And so am I for Ganymede. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | And so am I for [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | And so am I for no woman. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | (to [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]])<br>If this be so, why blame you me to [[motifs/love\|love]] you? |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | (to [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]])<br>If this be so, why blame you me to [[motifs/love\|love]] you? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | If this be so, why blame you me to [[motifs/love\|love]] you? |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Why do you speak too, ‘Why blame you me to [[motifs/love\|love]] you?’ |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | To her that is not here nor doth not hear. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Pray you, no more of this, ’tis like the howling of Irish wolves against the [[motifs/moon\|moon]]. (To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) I will help you if I can. (To [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) I would [[motifs/love\|love]] you if I could.—Tomorrow meet me all together. (To [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]]) I will marry you if ever I marry woman, and I’ll be married tomorrow. (To [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) I will satisfy you if ever I satisfy man, and you shall be married tomorrow. (To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) I will content you if what pleases you contents you, and you shall be married tomorrow. (To [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]]) As you [[motifs/love\|love]] [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]], meet. (To [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]]) As you [[motifs/love\|love]] [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]], meet. And as I [[motifs/love\|love]] no woman, I’ll meet. So fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. I have left you commands. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | I’ll not fail, if I live. |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | Nor I. |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | Nor I. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 5, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/touchstone\|Touchstone]] the clown and Audrey |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Tomorrow is the joyful day, Audrey, tomorrow will we be married. |
| AUDREY | I do [[motifs/desire\|desire]] it with all my heart; and I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] it is no dishonest [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to be a woman of the world. Here come two of the banished Duke’s pages. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter two Pages |
| FIRST PAGE | [[motifs/well\|Well]] met, honest gentleman. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | By my troth, [[motifs/well\|well]] met. Come, sit, sit, and a song. |
| SECOND PAGE | We are for you. Sit i’th’ middle. |
| FIRST PAGE | Shall we clap into’t roundly, without hawking, or spitting, or saying we are hoarse, which are the only prologues to a bad voice? |
| SECOND PAGE | I‘[[concepts/faith\|faith]], i’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], and both in a tune, like two gipsies on a [[motifs/horse\|horse]]. |
| BOTH PAGES | (sing)<br>It was a [[archetypes/lover\|lover]] and his lass,<br>With a hey, and a ho, and a hey-nonny-no,<br>That o’er the green cornfield did pass<br>In [[motifs/spring\|spring]]-[[motifs/time\|time]], the only pretty ring-[[motifs/time\|time]],<br>When [[motifs/birds\|birds]] do sing, hey ding-a-ding ding,<br>Sweet lovers [[motifs/love\|love]] the [[motifs/spring\|spring]].<br><br>Between the acres of the rye,<br>With a hey, and a ho, and a hey-nonny-no,<br>These pretty country folks would lie,<br>In [[motifs/spring\|spring]]-[[motifs/time\|time]], the only pretty ring-[[motifs/time\|time]],<br>When [[motifs/birds\|birds]] do sing, hey ding-a-ding ding,<br>Sweet lovers [[motifs/love\|love]] the [[motifs/spring\|spring]].<br><br>This carol they began that hour,<br>With a hey, and a ho, and a hey-nonny-no,<br>How that a life was but a flower,<br>In [[motifs/spring\|spring]]-[[motifs/time\|time]], the only pretty ring-[[motifs/time\|time]],<br>When [[motifs/birds\|birds]] do sing, hey ding-a-ding ding,<br><br>Sweet lovers [[motifs/love\|love]] the [[motifs/spring\|spring]].<br>And therefore take the present [[motifs/time\|time]],<br>With a hey, and a ho, and a hey-nonny-no,<br>For [[motifs/love\|love]] is crowned with the prime,<br>In [[motifs/spring\|spring]] [[motifs/time\|time]], the only pretty ring-[[motifs/time\|time]],<br>When [[motifs/birds\|birds]] do sing, hey ding-a-ding ding,<br>Sweet lovers [[motifs/love\|love]] the [[motifs/spring\|spring]]. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | Truly, young gentlemen, though there was no great matter in the ditty, yet the note was very untunable. |
| FIRST PAGE | You are deceived, sir, we kept [[motifs/time\|time]], we lost not our [[motifs/time\|time]]. |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | By my troth, yes, I count it but [[motifs/time\|time]] lost to hear such a foolish song. God b’wi’you, and God mend your voices. Come, Audrey. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |
| [[characters/touchstone\|TOUCHSTONE]] | severally |

### Atto 5, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/duke-senior\|Duke Senior]], Amiens, [[characters/jaques\|Jaques]], [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], [[characters/oliver\|Oliver]], and [[characters/celia\|Celia]] as Aliena |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | Dost thou believe, [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]], that the boy<br>Can do all this that he hath promised? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | I sometimes do believe, and sometimes do not,<br>As those that [[concepts/fear\|fear]] they [[concepts/hope\|hope]], and know they [[concepts/fear\|fear]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]] as Ganymede, with [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]] and |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | Patience once more, whiles our compact is urged.<br>(To the Duke) You say if I bring in your [[characters/rosalind\|Rosalind]]<br>You will bestow her on [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]] here? |
| [[characters/duke-senior\|DUKE SENIOR]] | That would I, had I kingdoms to give with her. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/orlando\|Orlando]])<br>And you say you will have her when I bring her? |
| [[characters/orlando\|ORLANDO]] | That would I, were I of all kingdoms [[archetypes/king\|king]]. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]])<br>You say you’ll marry me if I be willing? |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | That will I, should I die the hour after. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | But if you do refuse to marry me<br>You’ll give yourself to this most faithful shepherd? |
| [[characters/phoebe\|PHOEBE]] | So is the bargain. |
| [[characters/rosalind\|ROSALIND]] | (to [[characters/silvius\|Silvius]])<br>You say that you’ll have [[characters/phoebe\|Phoebe]] if she will. |
| [[characters/silvius\|SILVIUS]] | Though to have her and [[motifs/death\|death]] were both one thing. |
